10/10/24, 7:02 PM CCG - Anxiety - Self-Care Q6. Patient: How can you care for anxiety to keep it from getting worse and causing problems? (Check items understood.) QM Adhere to self-care plan Ask for help when feelings are overwhelming Exercise Follow a daily care plan Follow an eating (diet) plan Get all follow-up laboratory tests when due Keep a diary about feelings, activities, eating times, and medicines taken Keep all doctor appointments Keep surroundings calm (few people, simple surroundings, dim lighting) Know when to contact the doctor Manage situation or event triggers Manage stress Seek emergency care for signs of crisis plan not working Seek treatment for mood disorders Stop using alcohol, tobacco, or drugs Take medicines as ordered Other, specify: _______________________________________ Unknown [P1] Nothing [P1] Not all appropriate information known [P1] Q7. Inadequate social support Problems P1. Anxiety information needed Goal: Anxiety diagnosis better understood(4)(5)(6) Interventions: ASSIST: Identifying patient's goal priorities, willingness/ability to achieve goals, and preferences for communication and follow-up ASSIST: Goal setting that is specific, measurable, achievable, relevant, and with target completion time (eg, short/long- term, prioritized) ASSIST: Appointment scheduling EDUCATE: Definition(7)(8): Emotional disorder of distress with intense and potentially disabling anxiety reactions Most common of all behavioral health illnesses Types include generalized anxiety disorder (GAD), obsessive-compulsive disorder (OCD), panic disorder, phobic disorder (eg, social phobia), and posttraumatic stress disorder (PTSD). Occurs in absence of threatening situation (unlike fear) and can be response to perceived future threat Chronic (lifelong) in nature and interferes with life functions (eg, work, school) EDUCATE: Causes(8)(9): Biochemical influences Family history (genetics) Psychosocial and environmental influences Traumatic event history EDUCATE: Symptoms(8): https://careweb.careguidelines.com/ed28/ccg/iccg_246.htm#QualityMeasures_iccg_246?printview=1 12/24

Anxiolytic medication management needed Goal: Anxiolytic medication initiated and maintained(27)(37) Interventions: ASSIST: Appointment scheduling with prescribing provider for medication evaluation and refills EDUCATE: Purpose: Anxiolytics are used for treating acute anxiety and panic disorders. Optimal effect may take 2 weeks. Used as a short-term or occasional-use agent due to side effects and physical dependence concerns EDUCATE: Call provider immediately: Slurred speech; staggering; respiratory depression; severe depression, weakness, vertigo, or confusion EDUCATE: Side effects: Drowsiness, motor function impairment, urinary changes, confusion, vision changes, agitation Withdrawal symptoms may occur when discontinued (eg, depression, insomnia, anxiety, muscle cramps, tremors, vomiting, sweating, seizures, delirium). EDUCATE: Considerations: Use cautiously with glaucoma. SEND: Safety in the home - Fall protection (Spanish) SEND: Safety in the home - Fire and other risks (Spanish) P8. Atypical antipsychotic medication management needed Goal: Atypical antipsychotic medication initiated and maintained Interventions: ASSIST: Appointment scheduling with prescribing provider for medication evaluation and refills EDUCATE: Purpose: Atypical antipsychotics may be indicated for rapid control of acute mania to decrease agitation, aggression, and dangerous behavior.(38)(39) Second-generation antipsychotic medication May be indicated for second-line, third-line, or adjunctive therapies(40) EDUCATE: Call provider immediately: Neuroleptic malignant syndrome (NMS) (eg, extreme muscle rigidity, high fever, tachycardia, tachypnea, diaphoresis, stupor, fluctuations in blood pressure)[I](41) EDUCATE: Side effects: Difficulty urinating; jaundice; shuffling walk; fainting spells; seizures; pharyngitis; muscle spasms; excitement or restlessness; jerky movements of head, face, or neck; tachycardia; weight gain; blurred vision(42) Increased risk of metabolic side effects, including obesity, diabetes, and dyslipidemia(39) EDUCATE: Considerations(39): Use cautiously with history of seizures, liver disease, heart disease, blood disorders, or pregnancy.(26) Monitor blood glucose, cholesterol, and triglyceride levels. Selective serotonin reuptake inhibitors (SSRIs) or serotonin-norepinephrine reuptake inhibitors (SNRIs) medication management needed Goal: Selective serotonin reuptake inhibitors (SSRIs) or serotonin-norepinephrine reuptake inhibitors (SNRIs) medication initiated and maintained Interventions: ASSIST: Appointment scheduling with prescribing provider for medication evaluation and refills EDUCATE: Purpose: SSRIs and SNRIs are antidepressant medications.(43) Prevent anxiety symptoms, reduce relapse risk, and have less safety risks compared to benzodiazepines(44) Effects may take several weeks.(45) EDUCATE: Call provider immediately: If taking SSRIs and MAOIs or TCAs and serotonin syndrome signs present(46) (47): Serotonin syndrome (eg, tachycardia, diarrhea, hyperthermia, tremor, clonus, agitation, delirium) EDUCATE: Side effects: Agitation, headache, postural hypotension, sexual dysfunction (eg, abnormal ejaculation, impotence, loss of orgasm)(48) SNRIs have fewer sexual side effects than SSRIs. EDUCATE: Considerations(48)(49): Take dose early in the day to help avoid insomnia at night. Use cautiously with cardiac conditions due to possible elevation in blood pressure with SNRIs. Sleeping problems present Goal: Sleep improved Interventions: ASSIST: Identifying patient's goal priorities, willingness/ability to achieve goals, and preferences for communication and follow-up ASSIST: Goal setting that is specific, measurable, achievable, relevant, and with target completion time (eg, short/long- term, prioritized) ASSIST: Scheduling provider appointment for medical evaluation of sleep changes and creation of self-care plan to include(59)(60): Identifying techniques to improve sleep quality(61) Medication changes that can help patient sleep better (eg, changing the time stimulants are taken to early in the day or not taking diuretic before bedtime) Review of herbal preparations that may promote sleep(62) Creation of sleep diary(63) EDUCATE: Sleep hygiene techniques(59)(61)(64): Schedule sleep (go to bed and get up at same time every day). Spend no more than 8 hours per day in bed. Amount of average sleep time for adult is 7 to 8 hours. Limit napping to once a day for 20 to 30 minutes in early afternoon. Sleep in a dark, quiet bedroom. Use bedroom only for sleeping, not to watch TV, work, use electronics, or do other activities. Avoid pets in the bed. Get safe amount of sunlight every day and avoid bright lights before bedtime. Cut down on drinking fluids in the afternoon if nighttime urination is a problem. Avoid substances that may influence sleep (eg, caffeine, stimulants, alcohol). Stop smoking; nicotine is a stimulant. Get regular exercise, but avoid vigorous exercise 2 hours before bedtime.(65) Write down urgent thoughts if mind-racing is a problem. Do not eat big meals before bedtime; gastric process can cause sleep problems. Vaccinations not up to date, increasing infection risk Goal: Vaccinations up to date(68)(69) Interventions: ASSIST: Identifying patient's goal priorities, willingness/ability to achieve goals, and preferences for communication and follow-up ASSIST: Goal setting that is specific, measurable, achievable, relevant, and with target completion time (eg, short/long- term, prioritized) ASSIST: Appointment scheduling ASSIST: Identifying vaccines patient may need that are not contraindicated based on clinical status EDUCATE: Purpose and benefits of vaccinations EDUCATE: Recommended immunizations and timing of administration: Centers for Disease Control and Prevention's vaccination schedule: www.cdc.gov/vaccines(70) Vaccine schedules may need to be altered based on medications; check with the specialist provider for information.(71) Possible side effects or complications associated with getting vaccinated EDUCATE: Influenza vaccination annually(72): Side effects are mild and last 1 or 2 days. Common side effects with shot are sore, red, or swollen area where shot was given; fever; and aches. Common side effects with nasal spray are mild and last 1 or 2 days; they include runny nose, headache, sore throat, and cough. Allergic reaction rare and is usually caused by a chicken egg allergy EDUCATE: Pneumococcal vaccination potential side effects: Side effects are mild and last 1 or 2 days. Common side effects include a sore, red, or swollen area where shot was given; fever; and muscle aches. Serious side effects are rare. EDUCATE: COVID-19 vaccination potential side effects(70): Side effects are mild and last 1 or 2 days. Common side effects include a sore, red, or swollen area where shot was given; chills; fatigue; and aches. Serious side effects are rare. Programs should facilitate communication and collaboration with practitioners in a timely manner.(73)(75)(76)(77) Evidence: Systematic reviews and meta-analyses of randomized controlled trials have found psychotherapy (eg, cognitive behavioral therapy (CBT)) to have demonstrated efficacy for anxiety disorders in adults, children, and adolescents.(24) https://careweb.careguidelines.com/ed28/ccg/iccg_246.htm#QualityMeasures_iccg_246?printview=1 19/24
10/10/24, 7:02 PM CCG - Anxiety - Self-Care Evidence: The National Institute for Health and Care Excellence (NICE) recommends the use of individual cognitive behavioral therapy (CBT) as first-line treatment in adults for the treatment of social anxiety disorder.(32) National guidelines: The 2018 Physical Activity Guidelines Advisory Committee advises that adults with chronic conditions or disabilities, who are able, should follow the key activity guidelines for adults. The guidelines recommend that adults should do at least 150 to 300 minutes a week of moderate-intensity or 75 to 150 minutes a week of vigorous-intensity aerobic physical activity, or an equivalent combination of moderate-intensity and vigorous-intensity aerobic activity. They should also do muscle-strengthening activities on 2 or more days a week. Older adults should do multicomponent physical activity that includes balance training as well as aerobic and muscle-strengthening activities.(78) Quality standards: Standards indicate that programs should ensure that during care transitions the care plan is re-evaluated, modified, and communicated to appropriate care team members (eg, providers, caregivers) as needed to support the care transition.(73)(75)(76) (77) Transition consideration: Several studies demonstrated a reduction in 30-day hospital readmissions when the patient follow-up was within 7 days of hospital discharge.(79)(80)(81) Earlier follow-up (eg, within 48 hours after hospital discharge) may also help reduce hospital readmissions for high-risk or vulnerable patients.(15) Follow-up appointment attendance rates were higher when appointments were scheduled before hospital discharge.(82)(83) Outcomes: Several studies support the role of the pharmacist in medication reconciliation to identify and resolve pharmacy-related problems and improve outcomes (eg, prevention of medication-related adverse events, discontinuation of inappropriate medication). (84) Quality standards: Standards indicate that program content should address patient supports and psychosocial issues that may impede self-management. Programs should have a process for following up with patients on the status of referrals.(73) Quality standards: Standards indicate that program content should include a condition treatment plan, self-management and self- monitoring, and health behaviors (eg, sleep patterns). Programs should have a process for following up with patients on the status of referrals.(73) Specialty society: In a systematic review of the pharmacologic treatment of chronic insomnia in adults, the American Academy of Sleep Medicine does not recommend diphenhydramine, melatonin, tryptophan, or valerian for treating sleep onset or maintenance.(62) Specialty society: The American College of Physicians recommends cognitive behavioral therapy for insomnia as the first-line treatment for patients with chronic insomnia (trouble falling or staying asleep for 3 or more nights a week for at least 3 months, with daytime consequences). Cognitive behavioral therapy for insomnia generally includes cognitive therapy with sleep restriction, relaxation training, and stimulus control for 5 to 8 sessions.(85) References 1. Chiappetta L. Footnotes [A] Assess and address the emergency or urgent problems before continuing the engagement. [ A in Context Link 1 ] [B] A self-care plan tells you how to take care of your condition and when and where to get help for problems. [ B in Context Link 1 ] [C] Psychosocial interventions should be individualized and involve family and other supports as necessary.(8) [ C in Context Link 1 ] [D] Referral to a behavioral health specialist is based on an established treatment plan and needs at discharge from inpatient care. A psychiatrist serves as the leader of the inpatient team and can perform psychotherapy, medication prescription, and other somatic therapies. A clinical psychologist conducts individual, group, and family therapy, and administers and interprets psychological testing. A psychiatric social worker conducts individual, group, and family therapy addressing a patient's social needs to include placement, financial support, and community requirements.(86) [ D in Context Link 1 ] https://careweb.careguidelines.com/ed28/ccg/iccg_246.htm#QualityMeasures_iccg_246?printview=1 23/24
10/10/24, 7:02 PM CCG - Anxiety - Self-Care [E] Telehealth may improve access to and coordination of mental health care. Patients with behavioral health diagnoses may use telehealth or telepsychiatry to access mental health support or care (eg, to get assistance with problem-solving techniques or ask about presenting symptoms or medication use).(87) [ E in Context Link 1 ] [F] The timing of provider follow-up depends on the patient's disease severity, self-care complexity, patient/caregiver willingness and ability to provide patient care, and available social supports(88) [ F in Context Link 1 ] [G] Follow-up is recommended every 2 to 4 weeks for the first 3 months, then every 3 months to assess the patient's status.(20) [ G in Context Link 1 ] [H] The prevalence of either alcohol use or substance use disorder is approximately 16.5% over a 12-month period in patients with at least one anxiety disorder.(89) [ H in Context Link 1 ] [I] Neuroleptic malignant syndrome is a rare, rapidly progressive, potentially fatal complication more common with typical antipsychotics. Onset can occur hours or years after initiation of drug therapy.(41) [ I in Context Link 1 ] Codes ICD-10 Diagnosis: F06.4, F10.180, F10.280, F10.980, F12.180, F12.280, F12.980, F13.180, F13.280, F13.980, F14.180, F14.280, F14.980, F15.180, F15.280, F15.980, F16.180, F16.280, F16.980, F18.180, F18.280, F18.980, F19.180, F19.280, F19.980, F34.1, F40.10, F40.11, F40.8, F40.9, F41.0, F41.1, F41.3, F41.8, F41.9, F43.22, F43.23, F93.0, R45.88, Z91.51, Z91.52 [Hide] DSM-5: F06.4, F10.180, F10.280, F10.980, F12.180, F12.280, F12.980, F13.180, F13.280, F13.980, F14.180, F14.280, F14.980, F15.180, F15.280, F15.980, F16.180, F16.280, F16.980, F18.180, F18.280, F18.980, F19.180, F19.280, F19.980, F34.1, F40.10, F41.0, F41.1, F41.8, F41.9, F43.22, F43.23, F93.0, R45.88 [Hide] MCG Health Chronic Care 28th Edition Copyright  2024 MCG Health, LLC All Rights Reserved Last Update: 3/14/2024 1:50:38 AM Build Number: 28.1.20240313235330.016020 https://careweb.careguidelines.com/ed28/ccg/iccg_246.htm#QualityMeasures_iccg_246?printview=1 24/24

ADHD information needed Goal: ADHD diagnosis better understood(4)(5) Interventions: ASSIST: Identifying patient's goal priorities, willingness/ability to achieve goals, and preferences for communication and follow-up ASSIST: Goal setting that is specific, measurable, achievable, relevant, and with target completion time (eg, short/long- term, prioritized) ASSIST: Appointment scheduling EDUCATE: Definition: Attention-deficit hyperactivity disorder (ADHD) is a developmental neurobiological disorder.(6) It presents in childhood and can persist in varying degrees into adulthood, and is characterized by attention and/or hyperactivity and impulsivity problems. Symptoms may change in duration and intensity over time. EDUCATE: Causes: The cause of ADHD is not known. Factors that have been identified include biochemical influences and genetics.[C](6) EDUCATE: Symptoms(7): Behavioral changes (eg, sudden mood swings, aggression) Concentration difficulty Coping problems Emotional affect alteration (eg, flat, inappropriate, blunt, labile) Impulse control problems Social problems (eg, unpopular, argumentative) Thought process problems (eg, planning, reasoning, decision making) EDUCATE: Secondary complications of ADHD(6): Fewer close friends and trouble keeping those friendships Tend to have a negative perception of marital and family life and have a higher incidence of separation and divorce Problems with communication, roles, and problem solving Lower self-esteem Vocational challenges, such as frequent changes in employment (poor performance, quit or fired) EDUCATE: Diagnosis: Based on medical history, risk factors, physical examination, and diagnostic tests(8) EDUCATE: Treatment: ADL and IADL support Behavioral health therapy options Comorbid condition management https://careweb.careguidelines.com/ed28/ccg/iccg_248.htm?printview=1 12/23

ADHD medication management needed Goal: Medication information understood(8)(36) Interventions: ASSIST: Identifying patient's goal priorities, willingness/ability to achieve goals, and preferences for communication and follow-up ASSIST: Goal setting that is specific, measurable, achievable, relevant, and with target completion time (eg, short/long- term, prioritized) ASSIST: Appointment scheduling with provider for discussion on appropriate medication ASSIST: Providing reconciled medication list with all providers. See Medication Reconciliation Tool SR for more information. EDUCATE: Purpose: Importance of understanding what each medication is for, how it should be taken, and what to do if dose is missed Stimulant and nonstimulant medications Prescribed to increase cognition and attentiveness as well as to decrease distractibility, hyperactivity, and behavioral disruptions Optimal dose titration may take 4 to 6 weeks. Consequences of nonadherence (eg, to medication regimen)(37) EDUCATE: Medication indications, side effects, and considerations frequently change. See a drug reference for specific information not listed here. EDUCATE: Administration techniques: Route (eg, oral, intramuscular (IM), intravenous (IV)) With or without foods or liquids (eg, milk) Timing of administration Appropriate measuring device (eg, liquid medications) Proper storage EDUCATE: Get emergency help: Adverse or allergic reactions (eg, wheezing, chest tightness, fever, itching, bad cough, blue skin color, hemorrhage, seizures, swelling of face, lips, tongue, or throat) EDUCATE: Side effects: Management, and when to contact provider Common side effects include gastrointestinal changes, dry mouth, fatigue, insomnia, rash, dizziness, and headache. EDUCATE: Considerations: Discuss medication with provider if pregnant, planning a pregnancy, or breast-feeding. Comorbidities may be contraindications to the use of a drug (eg, liver disease, diabetes). Health checks, diary, and action plans (eg, blood pressure, weight, heart rate) Safety measures (eg, sun protection, driving, or operating machinery) Contact provider or pharmacist prior to taking any other over-the-counter or prescribed medication. Atomoxetine medication management needed Goal: Atomoxetine initiated and maintained(31)(38)(39) Interventions: ASSIST: Appointment scheduling with prescribing provider for medication evaluation and refills EDUCATE: Purpose: Atomoxetine is a nonstimulant medication used to reduce inattention, hyperactivity, and impulsivity.[H] First-line treatment with substance use comorbidities Therapeutic response may take 12 weeks. EDUCATE: Side effects: Somnolence, hypertension, nausea, abdominal pain, anorexia EDUCATE: Considerations: Avoid in patients with glaucoma. P7. Stimulant medication management needed Goal: Stimulant medication initiated and maintained(35) Interventions: ASSIST: Appointment scheduling with prescribing provider for medication evaluation and refills EDUCATE: Purpose: Stimulants may be indicated to reduce inattention, hyperactivity, and impulsivity.(38) Sustained-release and long-acting formulations available for dose simplification and increased adherence Drug holidays only under supervision of prescribing provider if growth suppression is a concern EDUCATE: Side effects: Anxiety, anorexia, hypertension, mood changes, psychotic symptoms, aggressive behavior, tremors, insomnia, drug-seeking behavior(7) EDUCATE: Considerations(7): Take after meals. Take dose early in the day to help avoid insomnia at night. Avoid large amounts of caffeine to prevent increase in restlessness and stimulation. Avoid medications containing stimulants such as caffeine or pseudoephedrine. Stimulants are controlled drugs and should be secured to prevent inappropriate use. Diversion of medications is illegal. Regular monitoring for medication adherence and limiting the risk of stimulant diversion or misuse Use cautiously if psychosis present.(40) SEND: Help with sleeping or insomnia (Spanish) P8. Nutritional value should be doubled, tripled, and so on based on the number of servings eaten. Serving size varies from product to product. 10/10/24, 7:03 PM CCG - Attention-Deficit Hyperactivity Disorder, Adult - Self-Care ASSIST: Identifying patient's goal priorities, willingness/ability to achieve goals, and preferences for communication and follow-up ASSIST: Goal setting that is specific, measurable, achievable, relevant, and with target completion time (eg, short/long- term, prioritized) ASSIST: Appointment scheduling ASSIST: Identifying vaccines patient may need that are not contraindicated based on clinical status EDUCATE: Purpose and benefits of vaccinations EDUCATE: Recommended immunizations and timing of administration: Centers for Disease Control and Prevention's vaccination schedule: www.cdc.gov/vaccines(53) Vaccine schedules may need to be altered based on medications; check with the specialist provider for information.(54) Possible side effects or complications associated with getting vaccinated EDUCATE: Influenza vaccination annually(55): Side effects are mild and last 1 or 2 days. Common side effects with shot are sore, red, or swollen area where shot was given; fever; and aches. Common side effects with nasal spray are mild and last 1 or 2 days; they include runny nose, headache, sore throat, and cough. Allergic reaction rare and is usually caused by a chicken egg allergy EDUCATE: Pneumococcal vaccination potential side effects: Side effects are mild and last 1 or 2 days. Common side effects include a sore, red, or swollen area where shot was given; fever; and muscle aches. Serious side effects are rare. EDUCATE: COVID-19 vaccination potential side effects(53): Side effects are mild and last 1 or 2 days. Common side effects include a sore, red, or swollen area where shot was given; chills; fatigue; and aches. Serious side effects are rare. 10/10/24, 7:03 PM CCG - Attention-Deficit Hyperactivity Disorder, Adult - Self-Care preferences (eg, culture, values, needs).(63) Quality standards: Standards indicate that program content should include an individualized self-care plan that includes patient preferences, prioritized goals, interventions with information on whom to contact in urgent situations, and scheduled follow-up. Programs should facilitate communication and collaboration with practitioners in a timely manner.(63)(64)(65)(66) National guidelines: The 2018 Physical Activity Guidelines Advisory Committee advises that adults with chronic conditions or disabilities, who are able, should follow the key activity guidelines for adults. The guidelines recommend that adults should do at least 150 to 300 minutes a week of moderate-intensity or 75 to 150 minutes a week of vigorous-intensity aerobic physical activity, or an equivalent combination of moderate-intensity and vigorous-intensity aerobic activity. They should also do muscle-strengthening activities on 2 or more days a week. Older adults should do multicomponent physical activity that includes balance training as well as aerobic and muscle-strengthening activities.(67) Quality standards: Standards indicate that programs should ensure that during care transitions the care plan is re-evaluated, modified, and communicated to appropriate care team members (eg, providers, caregivers) as needed to support the care transition.(63)(64)(65) (66) Transition consideration: Several studies demonstrated a reduction in 30-day hospital readmissions when the patient follow-up was within 7 days of hospital discharge.(68)(69)(70) Earlier follow-up (eg, within 48 hours after hospital discharge) may also help reduce hospital readmissions for high-risk or vulnerable patients.(15) Follow-up appointment attendance rates were higher when appointments were scheduled before hospital discharge.(71)(72) Outcomes: Several studies support the role of the pharmacist in medication reconciliation to identify and resolve pharmacy-related problems and improve outcomes (eg, prevention of medication-related adverse events, discontinuation of inappropriate medication). (73) National guidelines: The Institute of Medicine does not specify how much total fluid should come from water. Their recommendation is based on the median of total water intake on dietary surveys, which include water from liquids and solids. For healthy people, the advice is to consume at least 8 glasses (8 ounces (240 mL) each) of water daily; there is little evidence to support this recommendation. (74) Quality standards: Standards indicate that program content should address patient supports and psychosocial issues that may impede self-management. Programs should have a process for following up with patients on the status of referrals.(63) References 1. Chiappetta L. Mood disorders: depression. [ A in Context Link 1 ] [B] A self-care plan tells you how to take care of your condition and when and where to get help for problems. [ B in Context Link 1 ] [C] Nongenetic factors (eg, maternal smoking, prematurity, exposure to environmental lead or polychlorinated biphenyls) may also affect the pathogenesis of ADHD.(8)(75) [ C in Context Link 1 ] [D] Adults with ADHD may sustain an inner sense of restlessness. Attention span tends to increase with age, but still falls behind those who do not have the disorder.(7) [ D in Context Link 1 ] [E] Psychoeducational therapy focuses on the patient's understanding and awareness of their disorder.(29) Psychosocial therapy includes cognitive behavioral therapy (CBT), coaching, and counseling.(30) CBT provides techniques to change maladaptive cognitive and behavioral patterns.(29) It provides help in acquiring new skills. Young adults in college may benefit from coping skills, problem solving, organizational strategies, and distraction management.(29) [ E in Context Link 1 ] [F] Telehealth may improve access to and coordination of mental health care. Patients with behavioral health diagnoses may use telehealth or telepsychiatry to access mental health support or care (eg, to get assistance with problem-solving techniques or ask about presenting symptoms or medication use).(76) [ F in Context Link 1 ] [G] The timing of provider follow-up depends on the patient's disease severity, self-care complexity, patient/caregiver willingness and ability to provide patient care, and available social supports.(77) [ G in Context Link 1 ] [H] Nonstimulant medication (eg, atomoxetine) is an effective alternative with less potential for misuse.(38) [ H in Context Link 1 ] [I] Adequate intake is used when the recommended daily allowance cannot be determined because of insufficient data on requirements. Inadequate social support Problems P1. Bipolar disorder information needed Goal: Bipolar disorder diagnosis better understood(4)(5) Interventions: ASSIST: Identifying patient's goal priorities, willingness/ability to achieve goals, and preferences for communication and follow-up ASSIST: Goal setting that is specific, measurable, achievable, relevant, and with target completion time (eg, short/long- term, prioritized) ASSIST: Appointment scheduling EDUCATE: Definition: Bipolar disorders are a group of mood disorders characterized by unusual shifts in mood, energy, activity levels, and ability to carry out day-to-day tasks. It is also called manic-depressive disorder.(6)(7) Changes in mood may be gradual or rapid, and vary by individual. It is a severe lifelong illness that often leads to functional impairment and disability.(6) It can cause impairments of personal, educational, social, and occupational functioning. EDUCATE: Causes(6): Exact cause is unknown. Effects may take 2 to 8 weeks. Most people need to be on medication at least 18 to 24 months after adequate response to symptoms. Sleeping problems present Goal: Sleep improved Interventions: ASSIST: Identifying patient's goal priorities, willingness/ability to achieve goals, and preferences for communication and follow-up ASSIST: Goal setting that is specific, measurable, achievable, relevant, and with target completion time (eg, short/long- term, prioritized) ASSIST: Scheduling provider appointment for medical evaluation of sleep changes and creation of self-care plan to include(41)(42): Identifying techniques to improve sleep quality(43) Medication changes that can help patient sleep better (eg, changing the time stimulants are taken to early in the day or not taking diuretic before bedtime) Review of herbal preparations that may promote sleep(44) Creation of sleep diary(45) EDUCATE: Sleep hygiene techniques(41)(43)(46): Schedule sleep (go to bed and get up at same time every day). Spend no more than 8 hours per day in bed. Amount of average sleep time for adult is 7 to 8 hours. Limit napping to once a day for 20 to 30 minutes in early afternoon. Sleep in a dark, quiet bedroom. Use bedroom only for sleeping, not to watch TV, work, use electronics, or do other activities. Avoid pets in the bed. Get safe amount of sunlight every day and avoid bright lights before bedtime. Cut down on drinking fluids in the afternoon if nighttime urination is a problem. Avoid substances that may influence sleep (eg, caffeine, stimulants, alcohol). Stop smoking; nicotine is a stimulant. Get regular exercise, but avoid vigorous exercise 2 hours before bedtime.(47) Write down urgent thoughts if mind-racing is a problem. Do not eat big meals before bedtime; gastric process can cause sleep problems. EDUCATE: Herbal preparations to help sleep (eg, melatonin, tryptophan, valerian), as approved by provider(48) EDUCATE: Behavioral health treatment options and benefits (eg, cognitive behavioral therapy for insomnia)(45)(49) EDUCATE: Hypnotic medications use and misuse, if prescribed for insomnia(42) COORDINATE: Sleep specialist referral, if indicated(48) COORDINATE: Behavioral health services referral, if indicated(45) COORDINATE: Communication between care team members and care plan reconciliation(12)(14)(15) COORDINATE: Follow-up contact to evaluate sleep, status of referrals, and need for further assistance(16)(17) SEND: Help with sleeping or insomnia (Spanish) P11. Nutritional value should be doubled, tripled, and so on based on the number of servings eaten. Serving size varies from product to product. Form of food has impact on nutrition (eg, increased sugar content of orange juice vs one orange). Potential hazardous or at-risk alcohol use present Goal: Alcohol effects and complications of misuse understood(78) Interventions: ASSIST: Identifying patient's goal priorities, willingness/ability to achieve goals, and preferences for communication and follow-up ASSIST: Goal setting that is specific, measurable, achievable, relevant, and with target completion time (eg, short/long- term, prioritized) ASSIST: Appointment scheduling for evaluation of effects of alcohol use and self-care plan, if needed EDUCATE: Health effects of drinking(78)(79): Light to moderate intake (2 drinks per day for men and 1 drink per day for women) may be linked with poor health outcomes.[C](80)(81) Heavy drinking can cause acute alcohol poisoning and nutritional deficiencies. Long-term effects of alcohol misuse include cirrhosis, pancreatitis, peripheral neuropathy, and cancer.(82) EDUCATE: Follow drinking limits set by provider. EDUCATE: Comparison of personal drinking to national norms(79): Safe drinking levels for men (younger than 65 years) are 14 drinks or fewer per week and 4 drinks or fewer on one occasion. Safe drinking levels for women are 7 drinks or fewer per week and 3 drinks or fewer on one occasion. Safe drinking levels for healthy adults age 65 years are 7 drinks or fewer per week and 3 drinks or fewer on one occasion. No drinking during pregnancy is safe. Caution should be used when breast-feeding; consumption of a single drink may be safe if the mother waits 4 hours before breast-feeding or expresses breast milk for feeding prior to consuming the alcoholic drink. Dosage is individualized based on need and response of patient. If using patch, apply one new patch every 24 hours on skin that is dry, clean, and hairless. Rotate skin sites. Do not cut patch, and do not use more than one patch at a time. If using lozenge or gum, do not eat or drink for 15 minutes before or while utilizing. 10/10/24, 7:05 PM CCG - Bipolar Disorder - Self-Care Vaccine schedules may need to be altered based on medications; check with the specialist provider for information.(108) Possible side effects or complications associated with getting vaccinated EDUCATE: Influenza vaccination annually(109): Side effects are mild and last 1 or 2 days. Common side effects with shot are sore, red, or swollen area where shot was given; fever; and aches. Common side effects with nasal spray are mild and last 1 or 2 days; they include runny nose, headache, sore throat, and cough. Allergic reaction rare and is usually caused by a chicken egg allergy EDUCATE: Pneumococcal vaccination potential side effects: Side effects are mild and last 1 or 2 days. Common side effects include a sore, red, or swollen area where shot was given; fever; and muscle aches. Serious side effects are rare. EDUCATE: COVID-19 vaccination potential side effects(107): Side effects are mild and last 1 or 2 days. Common side effects include a sore, red, or swollen area where shot was given; chills; fatigue; and aches. Serious side effects are rare. COORDINATE: Community services referral, as needed COORDINATE: Vaccinations COORDINATE: Follow-up to evaluate vaccination understanding, referral status, and need for further assistance(16)(17) SEND: Coronavirus disease 2019 (COVID-19) vaccines (Spanish) SEND: Flu vaccine (Spanish) SEND: Pneumonia vaccine for adults (Spanish) SEND: Hepatitis A vaccine (Spanish) SEND: Hepatitis B vaccine (Spanish) Evidence Summary Quality standards: The Case Management Society of America's Standards of Practice for Case Management principles include facilitation of informed decision making, counseling, and health education with patient self-care that is responsive to the patient's preferences (eg, culture, values, needs).(110) Quality standards: Standards indicate that program content should include an individualized self-care plan that includes patient preferences, prioritized goals, interventions with information on whom to contact in urgent situations, and scheduled follow-up. Programs should facilitate communication and collaboration with practitioners in a timely manner.(110)(111)(112)(113) National guidelines: The 2018 Physical Activity Guidelines Advisory Committee advises that adults with chronic conditions or disabilities, who are able, should follow the key activity guidelines for adults. The guidelines recommend that adults should do at least 150 to 300 minutes a week of moderate-intensity or 75 to 150 minutes a week of vigorous-intensity aerobic physical activity, or an equivalent combination of moderate-intensity and vigorous-intensity aerobic activity. They should also do muscle-strengthening activities on 2 or more days a week. Older adults should do multicomponent physical activity that includes balance training as well as aerobic and muscle-strengthening activities.(114) Evidence: A randomized controlled study based on 526 patients found that smoking cessation can be achieved among people with severe mental illness without negatively impacting mental health (eg, depression, anxiety).(115) National guidelines: The National Institute for Health and Care Excellence (NICE) recommends offering supported employment programs to patients with bipolar disorder in primary or secondary care who wish to find or return to work.(10) Quality standards: Standards indicate that programs should ensure that during care transitions the care plan is re-evaluated, modified, and communicated to appropriate care team members (eg, providers, caregivers) as needed to support the care transition.(110)(111) (112)(113) Transition consideration: Several studies demonstrated a reduction in 30-day hospital readmissions when the patient follow-up was within 7 days of hospital discharge.(116)(117)(118) Earlier follow-up (eg, within 48 hours after hospital discharge) may also help reduce hospital readmissions for high-risk or vulnerable patients.(16) Follow-up appointment attendance rates were higher when appointments were scheduled before hospital discharge.(119)(120) Outcomes: Several studies support the role of the pharmacist in medication reconciliation to identify and resolve pharmacy-related problems and improve outcomes (eg, prevention of medication-related adverse events, discontinuation of inappropriate medication). (121) https://careweb.careguidelines.com/ed28/ccg/iccg_202.htm?printview=1 30/38
10/10/24, 7:05 PM CCG - Bipolar Disorder - Self-Care Quality standards: Standards indicate that program content should include a condition treatment plan, self-management and self- monitoring, and health behaviors (eg, sleep patterns). Programs should have a process for following up with patients on the status of referrals.(110) Specialty society: In a systematic review of the pharmacologic treatment of chronic insomnia in adults, the American Academy of Sleep Medicine does not recommend diphenhydramine, melatonin, tryptophan, or valerian for treating sleep onset or maintenance.(44) Specialty society: The American College of Physicians recommends cognitive behavioral therapy for insomnia as the first-line treatment for patients with chronic insomnia (trouble falling or staying asleep for 3 or more nights a week for at least 3 months, with daytime consequences). Cognitive behavioral therapy for insomnia generally includes cognitive therapy with sleep restriction, relaxation training, and stimulus control for 5 to 8 sessions.(122) Evidence: Multiple studies indicate that the large number and types of medications consumed by the elderly increase the risk of falls and should be evaluated.(41)(61)(123) National guidelines: The U.S. Preventive Services Task Force recommends exercise-based interventions to help reduce falls for people 65 years and older. Other interventions (eg, behavioral therapy, nutrition therapy, environmental modification, medication changes) may be considered based on the patient's preferences, the benefit-to-harm balance, and the circumstances of prior falls. Vitamin D supplementation is not recommended for fall prevention for older people without a known deficiency or osteoporosis.(52) National guidelines: The Institute of Medicine does not specify how much total fluid should come from water. Their recommendation is based on the median of total water intake on dietary surveys, which include water from liquids and solids. For healthy people, the advice is to consume at least 8 glasses (8 ounces (240 mL) each) of water daily; there is little evidence to support this recommendation. (124) Quality standards: Standards indicate that program content should address patient supports and psychosocial issues that may impede self-management. Programs should have a process for following up with patients on the status of referrals.(110) Evidence: The Alcohol Use Disorders Identification Test (AUDIT) questionnaire's 3-item subset of questions about alcohol consumption (AUDIT-C) is a brief screening test for problem drinking that has been validated in primary care office settings.(79) Quality standards: Standards indicate that program content should include a condition treatment plan, self-management and self- monitoring, and health behaviors (eg, substance use). Programs should have a process for following up with patients on the status of referrals.(110) National guidelines: The US Department of Health and Human Services' Dietary Guidelines for Americans suggests that moderate alcohol consumption (2 drinks/day in men or 1 drink/day in women) may increase the overall risk of death from various causes, such as several types of cancer and some forms of cardiovascular disease.(65) Specialty society: The American Heart Association does not support initiation of alcohol intake to improve cardiovascular health.(80) National guidelines: The U.S. Preventive Services Task Force (USPSTF) recommends that clinicians ask all adults about tobacco use, advise them to stop using tobacco, and provide behavioral interventions and FDA-approved pharmacotherapy for cessation to nonpregnant adults who use tobacco. The USPSTF recommends that clinicians ask all pregnant persons about tobacco use, advise them to stop using tobacco, and provide behavioral interventions for cessation to those who use tobacco.(125) National guidelines: The U.S. Preventive Services Task Force (USPSTF) concludes that there is insufficient evidence to determine the balance of benefits and harms of primary care interventions for tobacco cessation among school-age children and adolescents who already smoke because of a lack of adequately powered studies on behavioral counseling interventions and a lack of studies on medications. Clinical judgment should be used to decide how to best help youth who use tobacco.(126) Evidence: A systematic review of 33 trials with 21,600 participants found that incentives (eg, money, vouchers) improved smoking cessation rates at long-term follow-up (at least 6 months) in mixed-population studies. Ten trials of 2571 pregnant women found that women in the incentive-rewards group were more likely to stop smoking.(127) Evidence: A systematic review of 47 studies with more than 18,000 people showed improved smoking cessation with increased therapeutic support and nicotine replacement therapy (NRT) as compared with NRT alone.(99) Evidence: A review of 43 studies with more than 28,000 people found with moderate certainty that when no other support is available, written self-help materials help more people to stop smoking compared with getting no help at all.(128) Evidence: A meta-analysis of 10 randomized studies of 3655 participants using nicotine replacement therapy found that behavioral support focusing on adherence to smoking cessation medications can modestly improve adherence.(129) Evidence: A network meta-analysis of 33 systematic reviews with 250,563 participants found that behavioral support for smoking cessation can increase quit rates at 6 months or longer, with the strongest benefit for the provision of any type of counseling and https://careweb.careguidelines.com/ed28/ccg/iccg_202.htm?printview=1 31/38
10/10/24, 7:05 PM CCG - Bipolar Disorder - Self-Care guaranteed financial incentives.(130) Evidence: A systematic review of 136 trials of nicotine replacement therapy (NRT) with 64,640 people found that all forms of NRT made it more likely that a person's attempt to quit smoking would succeed. Their chances of stopping smoking increased by 50% to 60% with or without additional counseling. There is no evidence that NRT increases the risk of heart attacks. In pregnant women, evidence suggests that NRT can increase the chance of quitting at the time of delivery.(131) Evidence: A systematic review of 24 trials with 7279 participants found no evidence that adding exercise to smoking cessation support improves abstinence compared with support alone, but the evidence is insufficient to assess whether there is a modest benefit.(132) Evidence: A systematic review of 51 studies including 22,000 people who smoked tobacco found that cessation was more likely if smokers cut down their intake when using stop-smoking medication (eg, varenicline) or fast-acting nicotine replacement therapy vs quitting all at once.(97) Specialty Society: The American Thoracic Society recommendations for tobacco-dependent adults include using varenicline rather than a nicotine patch, using varenicline rather than bupropion, using varenicline rather than a nicotine patch in adults with a comorbid psychiatric condition, initiating varenicline in adults even if they are unready to quit, and using controller therapy for an extended treatment duration greater than 12 weeks.(102) References 1. [ A in Context Link 1 ] [B] A self-care plan tells you how to take care of your condition and when and where to get help for problems. [ B in Context Link 1 ] [C] A standard drink consists of 0.6 ounces (18 mL) of ethanol, which is contained in 12 ounces (360 mL) of beer (alcohol by volume (ABV) = 5%), 5 ounces (150 mL) of wine (ABV = 12%), and 1.5 ounces (45 mL) of spirits (ABV = 40%).(65) [ C in Context Link 1, 2 ] [D] Electroconvulsive therapy (ECT) may be useful for severe and psychotic depression, particularly if the patient is not responsive to pharmacologic treatment.(10)(21)(133) [ D in Context Link 1 ] [E] Telehealth may improve access to and coordination of mental health care. Patients with behavioral health diagnoses may use telehealth or telepsychiatry to access mental health support or care (eg, to get assistance with problem-solving techniques or ask about presenting symptoms or medication use).(134) [ E in Context Link 1 ] [F] Psychosocial interventions should be individualized and involve family and other supports as necessary.(10) [ F in Context Link 1 ] [G] Referral to a behavioral health specialist is based on an established treatment plan and needs at discharge from inpatient care. A psychiatrist serves as the leader of the inpatient team and can perform psychotherapy, medication prescription, and other somatic therapies. A clinical psychologist conducts individual, group, and family therapy, and administers and interprets psychological testing. A psychiatric social worker conducts individual, group, and family therapy addressing a patient's social needs to include placement, financial support, and community requirements.(29) [ G in Context Link 1 ] [H] The timing of provider follow-up depends on the patient's disease severity, self-care complexity, patient/caregiver willingness and ability to provide patient care, and available social supports.(135) [ H in Context Link 1 ] [I] People with chronic behavioral health conditions have higher rates of medical illness and higher mortality vs the general population. (8) [ I in Context Link 1 ] [J] Medications should be regularly reviewed to eliminate medications used for acute episodes.(10) [ J in Context Link 1 ] [K] Neuroleptic malignant syndrome is a rare, rapidly progressive, potentially fatal complication more common with typical antipsychotics. Onset can occur hours or years after initiation of drug therapy.(38) [ K in Context Link 1 ] [L] Fall risk factors include mobility or functional impairment, poor health, weakness, incontinence, polypharmacy (4 or more medications), medication dose changes, or patient taking substances that increase risk (eg, benzodiazepines, sedative-hypnotics, antidepressants, antipsychotics, alcohol, opiates).(61)(136) [ L in Context Link 1 ] [M] A pharmacist can support medication reconciliation for redundancy or duplication and identify drug interactions (eg, with other drugs, food).(58) [ M in Context Link 1 ] [N] Services to consider for community support include stairway modifications, widening of interior doors for ambulation equipment, and installation of handrails.(50) [ N in Context Link 1 ] [O] Adequate intake is used when the recommended daily allowance cannot be determined because of insufficient data on requirements.(66) [ O in Context Link 1 ] [P] Primary pharmacotherapy for tobacco dependence includes varenicline, bupropion, nicotine gum, nicotine inhaler, nicotine nasal spray, and nicotine patch. Nicotine replacement therapies (NRTs) should be used with caution for patients in the 2 weeks following myocardial infarction and for those with life-threatening arrhythmias or severe angina.(94)(125) [ P in Context Link 1 ] https://careweb.careguidelines.com/ed28/ccg/iccg_202.htm?printview=1 37/38

10/10/24, 7:05 PM CCG - Dementia - Self-Care Problems P1. Dementia information needed Goal: Dementia diagnosis better understood(4)(5) Interventions: ASSIST: Identifying patient's goal priorities, willingness/ability to achieve goals, and preferences for communication and follow-up ASSIST: Goal setting that is specific, measurable, achievable, relevant, and with target completion time (eg, short/long- term, prioritized) ASSIST: Appointment scheduling EDUCATE: Definition: Dementia is a progressive and degenerative central nervous system disorder characterized by cognitive decline.(6)(7) Dementia is associated with a progressive loss of functional skills in daily living, affecting memory, thinking, language, judgment, and behavior. Depression may produce a rapid onset of cognitive deficits; therefore, dementia may present as depression, or it may co-occur with depression.(8) EDUCATE: Dementia types(9): Alzheimer disease (AD) Vascular dementia (VaD) Dementia with Lewy bodies (DLB) Other neurodegenerative dementia EDUCATE: Causes(8): Alcoholism Alzheimer disease Brain tumors Depression (modifiable risk factor) Head injuries Hydrocephalus Infection: neurosyphilis, HIV Medications (modifiable risk factor): anticholinergics, barbiturates, bromides, glucocorticoids, haloperidol, opiates Metals: lead, mercury Vitamin deficiency: B12, thiamine EDUCATE: Symptoms(7)(9): Emotional affect alteration (eg, flat, inappropriate, blunt, labile) Cognitive changes (eg, confusion, memory loss) Depression Agitation Concentration difficulty Gait, motor, and balance problems Hallucinations Hygiene poor Impulse control problems Thought process problems (eg, disorganized speech, trouble completing activities, concentration difficulty) Delusions (eg, paranoid) EDUCATE: Diagnosis[D](7): Based on medical history, risk factors, physical examination, and diagnostic tests Behavioral health examination and history Positive screening instrument EDUCATE: Risk factors(6)(10): Varies by type and age of onset; result of multiple factors rather than a single cause Age is a significant factor for Alzheimer disease, with incidence doubling after age 65 years. Cardiovascular disease and factors that increase risk of cardiovascular disease (ie, smoking, obesity, hypertension) First-degree relative with Alzheimer disease or genetic predisposition Long-term neurologic conditions such as Parkinson disease Traumatic brain injury EDUCATE: Treatment(8)(11): ADL and IADL support Behavioral health therapy options Comorbid condition management End-of-life or palliative care Medication management Medications for pre-existing conditions may need to be changed (type or dose).(12) https://careweb.careguidelines.com/ed28/ccg/iccg_250.htm?printview=1 17/42

10/10/24, 7:05 PM CCG - Dementia - Self-Care Proper storage EDUCATE: Caregiver's medication administration information(65): Supervise patient taking medication. Remove medication from sight once taken. Maintain safety. Monitor side effects. Report problems to healthcare provider. Establish backup plan to maintain continuity of medication regimen. Transdermal patch management - practice good skin-washing technique after removal of transdermal patch to prevent overdosing. EDUCATE: Get emergency help: Adverse or allergic reactions (eg, wheezing, chest tightness, fever, itching, bad cough, blue skin color, hemorrhage, seizures, swelling of face, lips, tongue, or throat) EDUCATE: Side effects: Management, and when to contact provider Common side effects include gastrointestinal changes, dry mouth, fatigue, insomnia, rash, dizziness, and headache. Comorbidities may be contraindications to the use of a drug (eg, liver disease, diabetes). Aspiration risk evaluation needed Goal: Aspiration risk evaluated and management plan initiated(91) Interventions: ASSIST: Identifying patient's goal priorities, willingness/ability to achieve goals, and preferences for communication and follow-up ASSIST: Goal setting that is specific, measurable, achievable, relevant, and with target completion time (eg, short/long- term, prioritized) ASSIST: Appointment scheduling ASSIST: Self-care plan creation between patient and provider (eg, goals, management, treating symptoms, getting help) EDUCATE: Reducing aspiration risk and complications(77)(92): Frequent small meals and small bites when eating If patient has one-sided facial weakness, place food on other side of mouth. Oral hygiene before meals to stimulate taste Postpone meal if fatigued. Avoiding excessive sedation during mealtimes Upright position for feedings and medications. Keep upright for 30 minutes after meals. Reduce distractions. Alternate solids with fluids while eating. Caregivers should feed patient slowly and monitor patient for pocketing food, aspiration, or choking. Training in Heimlich maneuver and CPR for caregivers Consider size, shape, taste, and texture of medications. Alternatives may be needed (eg, liquid, modified-release capsules, or enteric-coated tablets or capsules). EDUCATE: Meal planning(93): Selecting foods within care team recommendation (eg, mashed potatoes for soft foods) Food preparation ideas based on diet recommendations EDUCATE: Ways to improve swallowing ability(94): Change in body position Swallowing exercises Avoid difficult-to-swallow foods (eg, peanut butter or caramel). EDUCATE: Feeding techniques(93): Give small bites, or make sure patient takes small bites. Feed slowly, or make sure patient eats slowly. Alternate solids and liquids while feeding. Check for food in patient's mouth before giving additional bites. EDUCATE: When to get emergency help(77)(94): Aspiration (eg, coughing, choking, hypoxia, wheezing) Respiratory impairment (eg, gasping for air, choking, cyanosis, chest pain, severe dyspnea)(95) Trouble talking EDUCATE: When to call the provider(77)(96): Swallowing changes (eg, pain, difficulty, new-onset choking or coughing) Drooling when eating or drinking Food comes up when you try to swallow. Sleeping problems present Goal: Sleep improved Interventions: ASSIST: Identifying patient's goal priorities, willingness/ability to achieve goals, and preferences for communication and follow-up ASSIST: Goal setting that is specific, measurable, achievable, relevant, and with target completion time (eg, short/long- term, prioritized) ASSIST: Scheduling provider appointment for medical evaluation of sleep changes and creation of self-care plan to include(80)(98): Identifying techniques to improve sleep quality(99) Medication changes that can help patient sleep better (eg, changing the time stimulants are taken to early in the day or not taking diuretic before bedtime) Review of herbal preparations that may promote sleep(100) Creation of sleep diary(101) EDUCATE: Sleep hygiene techniques(80)(99)(102): Schedule sleep (go to bed and get up at same time every day). Spend no more than 8 hours per day in bed. Amount of average sleep time for adult is 7 to 8 hours. Limit napping to once a day for 20 to 30 minutes in early afternoon. Sleep in a dark, quiet bedroom. Use bedroom only for sleeping, not to watch TV, work, use electronics, or do other activities. Avoid pets in the bed. Get safe amount of sunlight every day and avoid bright lights before bedtime. Cut down on drinking fluids in the afternoon if nighttime urination is a problem. Avoid substances that may influence sleep (eg, caffeine, stimulants, alcohol). Stop smoking; nicotine is a stimulant. Get regular exercise, but avoid vigorous exercise 2 hours before bedtime.(103) Write down urgent thoughts if mind-racing is a problem. Do not eat big meals before bedtime; gastric process can cause sleep problems. EDUCATE: Herbal preparations to help sleep (eg, melatonin, tryptophan, valerian), as approved by provider(104) EDUCATE: Behavioral health treatment options and benefits (eg, cognitive behavioral therapy for insomnia)(101)(105) EDUCATE: Hypnotic medications use and misuse, if prescribed for insomnia(98) COORDINATE: Sleep specialist referral, if indicated(104) COORDINATE: Behavioral health services referral, if indicated(101) COORDINATE: Communication between care team members and care plan reconciliation(15)(17)(18) COORDINATE: Follow-up contact to evaluate sleep, status of referrals, and need for further assistance(19)(20) SEND: Help with sleeping or insomnia (Spanish) P14. 10/10/24, 7:05 PM CCG - Dementia - Self-Care ASSIST: Appointment scheduling with prescribing provider for medication evaluation and refills EDUCATE: Purpose: Medication is used to reduce withdrawal feeling during smoking cessation. Dosage is individualized based on need and response of patient. If using patch, apply one new patch every 24 hours on skin that is dry, clean, and hairless. Rotate skin sites. Do not cut patch, and do not use more than one patch at a time. If using lozenge or gum, do not eat or drink for 15 minutes before or while utilizing. EDUCATE: Call provider immediately: Nicotine toxicity (eg, nausea, abdominal pain, vomiting, diarrhea, sweating, disturbed vision or hearing, palpitations, altered respirations) EDUCATE: Side effects: Anxiety, sleep disorders, depression, myalgia Patch may cause redness or swelling of the skin around the patch that does not go away. 10/10/24, 7:05 PM CCG - Dementia - Self-Care Safe drinking levels for women are 7 drinks or fewer per week and 3 drinks or fewer on one occasion. Caution should be used when breast-feeding; consumption of a single drink may be safe if the mother waits 4 hours before breast-feeding or expresses breast milk for feeding prior to consuming the alcoholic drink. The dementia care manager (nurse practitioner) partnered with primary care physicians, specialty physicians, and community-based organizations to provide education and support, which showed improvement with behavioral symptoms in persons with dementia as well as a decrease in caregiver distress.(140) Quality standards: Standards indicate that program content should include an individualized self-care plan that includes patient preferences, prioritized goals, interventions with information on whom to contact in urgent situations, and scheduled follow-up. Programs should facilitate communication and collaboration with practitioners in a timely manner.(139)(141)(142)(143) National guidelines: The 2018 Physical Activity Guidelines Advisory Committee advises that adults with chronic conditions or disabilities, who are able, should follow the key activity guidelines for adults. The guidelines recommend that adults should do at least 150 to 300 minutes a week of moderate-intensity or 75 to 150 minutes a week of vigorous-intensity aerobic physical activity, or an equivalent combination of moderate-intensity and vigorous-intensity aerobic activity. They should also do muscle-strengthening activities on 2 or more days a week. Older adults should do multicomponent physical activity that includes balance training as well as aerobic and muscle-strengthening activities.(144) Evidence: Cognitive stimulation therapy, which encourages information processing in a pleasurable context and can be administered in either a group or individual format, has been shown in studies to improve quality of life, modestly improve cognitive functioning, and slow cognitive decline in patients with mild to moderate dementia above and beyond any benefits that may be achieved with medication therapy.(38) Evidence: A systematic review of 33 randomized controlled trials found that cognitive therapy in patients with mild to moderate dementia may be associated with positive effects on global cognition and verbal semantic fluency at the end of treatment.(145) Evidence: A systematic review of 37 studies including 2766 participants with mild or moderate dementia found that participants in cognitive stimulation programs improved on measures of communication and social interactions and showed slight benefits in day-to- day activities and in their own perceptions of their mood.(146) Specialty society: The National Institute for Health and Care Excellence (NICE) advises against the use of ginseng, vitamin E supplements, or herbal formulations to treat dementia.(38) Specialty society: The American Geriatrics Society does not recommend feeding tubes in older adults with advanced dementia. The society states that tube feedings are associated with agitation, greater use of physical and chemical restraints, greater healthcare use related to tube complications, and development of new pressure injuries. Careful hand feeding should be offered, as outcomes of death, aspiration pneumonia, functional status, and comfort are as good as with tube feedings.(147) Quality standards: Standards indicate that programs should ensure that during care transitions the care plan is re-evaluated, modified, and communicated to appropriate care team members (eg, providers, caregivers) as needed to support the care transition.(139)(141) (142)(143) Evidence: Comorbid psychiatric disorders (eg, depression, psychosis, and anxiety), as well as acute behavioral disturbances (eg, agitation), are common in patients with dementia and have a significant impact on patient quality of life, caregiver stress, and the likelihood of institutionalization.(9)(46) https://careweb.careguidelines.com/ed28/ccg/iccg_250.htm?printview=1 33/42
10/10/24, 7:05 PM CCG - Dementia - Self-Care Transition consideration: Several studies demonstrated a reduction in 30-day hospital readmissions when the patient follow-up was within 7 days of hospital discharge.(148)(149)(150) Earlier follow-up (eg, within 48 hours after hospital discharge) may also help reduce hospital readmissions for high-risk or vulnerable patients.(19) Follow-up appointment attendance rates were higher when appointments were scheduled before hospital discharge.(151)(152) Outcomes: Several studies support the role of the pharmacist in medication reconciliation to identify and resolve pharmacy-related problems and improve outcomes (eg, prevention of medication-related adverse events, discontinuation of inappropriate medication). (153) Evidence: A systematic review and meta-analysis including 44 randomized controlled trials and 9811 patients with dementia found a small clinical benefit for memantine (with or without concomitant cholinesterase inhibitors) in moderate to severe Alzheimer disease in clinical global rating, cognitive function, and performance in activities of daily living.(154) A review of the effects of memantine on clinical progression in Alzheimer disease concluded that memantine may slow the decline in function, and in some cases symptoms may improve.(64) National guidelines: The National Institute for Health and Care Excellence (NICE) recommends that patients with dementia try antipsychotics only if severely distressed or at risk of harming themselves or others, and they try them along with other interventions to relieve distress.(69) Evidence: Current evidence suggests that antipsychotics are best reserved as a last resort for severe refractory aggression, agitation, or psychosis, or when a serious threat of immediate harm or safety risk exists.(12) National guidelines: The U.S. Preventive Services Task Force recommends exercise interventions (eg, gait training, balance training) to help prevent falls in at-risk adults age 65 years or older.(81) National guidelines: The U.S. Preventive Services Task Force recommends exercise-based interventions to help reduce falls for people 65 years and older. Other interventions (eg, behavioral therapy, nutrition therapy, environmental modification, medication changes) may be considered based on the patient's preferences, the benefit-to-harm balance, and the circumstances of prior falls. Vitamin D supplementation is not recommended for fall prevention for older people without a known deficiency or osteoporosis.(81) Quality standards: Standards indicate that program content should include a condition treatment plan, self-management and self- monitoring, and health behaviors (eg, sleep patterns). Programs should have a process for following up with patients on the status of referrals.(139) Specialty society: In a systematic review of the pharmacologic treatment of chronic insomnia in adults, the American Academy of Sleep Medicine does not recommend diphenhydramine, melatonin, tryptophan, or valerian for treating sleep onset or maintenance. (100) Specialty society: The American College of Physicians recommends cognitive behavioral therapy for insomnia as the first-line treatment for patients with chronic insomnia (trouble falling or staying asleep for 3 or more nights a week for at least 3 months, with daytime consequences). Cognitive behavioral therapy for insomnia generally includes cognitive therapy with sleep restriction, relaxation training, and stimulus control for 5 to 8 sessions.(155) National guidelines: The Institute of Medicine does not specify how much total fluid should come from water. Their recommendation is based on the median of total water intake on dietary surveys, which include water from liquids and solids. For healthy people, the advice is to consume at least 8 glasses (8 ounces (240 mL) each) of water daily; there is little evidence to support this recommendation. (156) Evidence: The Alcohol Use Disorders Identification Test (AUDIT) questionnaire's 3-item subset of questions about alcohol consumption (AUDIT-C) is a brief screening test for problem drinking that has been validated in primary care office settings.(129) Quality standards: Standards indicate that program content should include a condition treatment plan, self-management and self- monitoring, and health behaviors (eg, substance use). Programs should have a process for following up with patients on the status of referrals.(139) National guidelines: The U.S. Preventive Services Task Force (USPSTF) recommends that clinicians ask all adults about tobacco use, advise them to stop using tobacco, and provide behavioral interventions and FDA-approved pharmacotherapy for cessation to nonpregnant adults who use tobacco. The USPSTF recommends that clinicians ask all pregnant persons about tobacco use, advise them to stop using tobacco, and provide behavioral interventions for cessation to those who use tobacco.(157) National guidelines: The U.S. Preventive Services Task Force (USPSTF) concludes that there is insufficient evidence to determine the balance of benefits and harms of primary care interventions for tobacco cessation among school-age children and adolescents who already smoke because of a lack of adequately powered studies on behavioral counseling interventions and a lack of studies on medications. Clinical judgment should be used to decide how to best help youth who use tobacco.(158) https://careweb.careguidelines.com/ed28/ccg/iccg_250.htm?printview=1 34/42
10/10/24, 7:05 PM CCG - Dementia - Self-Care Evidence: A systematic review of 33 trials with 21,600 participants found that incentives (eg, money, vouchers) improved smoking cessation rates at long-term follow-up (at least 6 months) in mixed-population studies. Ten trials of 2571 pregnant women found that women in the incentive-rewards group were more likely to stop smoking.(159) Evidence: A systematic review of 47 studies with more than 18,000 people showed improved smoking cessation with increased therapeutic support and nicotine replacement therapy (NRT) as compared with NRT alone.(122) Evidence: A review of 43 studies with more than 28,000 people found with moderate certainty that when no other support is available, written self-help materials help more people to stop smoking compared with getting no help at all.(160) Evidence: A meta-analysis of 10 randomized studies of 3655 participants using nicotine replacement therapy found that behavioral support focusing on adherence to smoking cessation medications can modestly improve adherence.(161) Evidence: A network meta-analysis of 33 systematic reviews with 250,563 participants found that behavioral support for smoking cessation can increase quit rates at 6 months or longer, with the strongest benefit for the provision of any type of counseling and guaranteed financial incentives.(162) Evidence: A systematic review of 136 trials of nicotine replacement therapy (NRT) with 64,640 people found that all forms of NRT made it more likely that a person's attempt to quit smoking would succeed. Their chances of stopping smoking increased by 50% to 60% with or without additional counseling. There is no evidence that NRT increases the risk of heart attacks. In pregnant women, evidence suggests that NRT can increase the chance of quitting at the time of delivery.(163) Evidence: A systematic review of 24 trials with 7279 participants found no evidence that adding exercise to smoking cessation support improves abstinence compared with support alone, but the evidence is insufficient to assess whether there is a modest benefit.(164) Evidence: A systematic review of 51 studies including 22,000 people who smoked tobacco found that cessation was more likely if smokers cut down their intake when using stop-smoking medication (eg, varenicline) or fast-acting nicotine replacement therapy vs quitting all at once.(119) Specialty Society: The American Thoracic Society recommendations for tobacco-dependent adults include using varenicline rather than a nicotine patch, using varenicline rather than bupropion, using varenicline rather than a nicotine patch in adults with a comorbid psychiatric condition, initiating varenicline in adults even if they are unready to quit, and using controller therapy for an extended treatment duration greater than 12 weeks.(125) National guidelines: The US Department of Health and Human Services' Dietary Guidelines for Americans suggests that moderate alcohol consumption (2 drinks/day in men or 1 drink/day in women) may increase the overall risk of death from various causes, such as several types of cancer and some forms of cardiovascular disease.(110) Specialty society: The American Heart Association does not support initiation of alcohol intake to improve cardiovascular health.(130) References 1. 10/10/24, 7:05 PM CCG - Dementia - Self-Care [F] Vascular dementia is associated with hypertension, transient ischemic attacks (TIAs), and cardiovascular disease and has the same preventive measures and risk factors (eg, hypercholesterolemia, heart disease, diabetes).(27)(40) [ F in Context Link 1 ] [G] The Mediterranean diet, which recommends fish intake, is associated with decreased risk of Alzheimer disease (AD) and decreased risk of converting from mild cognitive impairment (MCI) to AD dementia.(166) [ G in Context Link 1 ] [H] The timing of provider follow-up depends on the patient's disease severity, self-care complexity, patient/caregiver willingness and ability to provide patient care, and available social supports.(167) [ H in Context Link 1 ] [I] Rule out all obvious causes of agitation (eg, pain) and provide behavioral interventions prior to administration of typical antipsychotic medication.(69)(70) [ I in Context Link 1 ] [J] Neuroleptic malignant syndrome is a rare, rapidly progressive, potentially fatal complication more common with typical antipsychotics. Onset can occur hours or years after initiation of drug therapy.(71) [ J in Context Link 1 ] [K] Tardive dyskinesia (TD) is more common with first-generation (typical) antipsychotics. The risk of TD is higher with long-term use of antipsychotics. The offending medication should be removed with the first sign of symptoms, as symptoms may be irreversible.(168) [ K in Context Link 1 ] [L] Use typical antipsychotic medication when psychotic symptoms outweigh the adverse effects of medication.(72) [ L in Context Link 1 ] [M] A pharmacist can support medication reconciliation for redundancy or duplication and identify drug interactions (eg, with other drugs, food).(73) [ M in Context Link 1 ] [N] Services to consider for community support include stairway modifications, widening of interior doors for ambulation equipment, and installation of handrails.(106) [ N in Context Link 1 ] [O] Many driver rehabilitation programs provide therapy and behind-the-wheel assessments. Therapy assessment covers motor, cognitive, perception, and reaction times. The behind-the-wheel assessment covers fitness to drive.(169) [ O in Context Link 1 ] [P] Adequate intake is used when the recommended daily allowance cannot be determined because of insufficient data on requirements.(111) [ P in Context Link 1 ] [Q] Primary pharmacotherapy for tobacco dependence includes varenicline, bupropion, nicotine gum, nicotine inhaler, nicotine nasal spray, and nicotine patch. Nicotine replacement therapies (NRTs) should be used with caution for patients in the 2 weeks following myocardial infarction and for those with life-threatening arrhythmias or severe angina.(116)(157) [ Q in Context Link 1 ] Codes ICD-10 Diagnosis: F01.50, F01.511, F01.518, F01.52, F01.53, F01.54, F01.A0, F01.A11, F01.A18, F01.A2, F01.A3, F01.A4, F01.B0, F01.B11, F01.B18, F01.B2, F01.B3, F01.B4, F01.C0, F01.C11, F01.C18, F01.C2, F01.C3, F01.C4, F02.80, F02.811, F02.818, F02.82, F02.83, F02.84, F02.A0, F02.A11, F02.A18, F02.A2, F02.A3, F02.A4, F02.B0, F02.B11, F02.B18, F02.B2, F02.B3, F02.B4, F02.C0, F02.C11, F02.C18, F02.C2, F02.C3, F02.C4, F03.90, F03.911, F03.918, F03.92, F03.93, F03.94, F03.A0, F03.A11, F03.A18, F03.A2, F03.A3, F03.A4, F03.B0, F03.B11, F03.B18, F03.B2, F03.B3, F03.B4, F03.C0, F03.C11, F03.C18, F03.C2, F03.C3, F03.C4, F06.70, F06.71, F10.27, F10.97, F13.27, F13.97, F18.17, F18.27, F18.97, F19.17, F19.27, F19.97, G30.0, G30.1, G30.8, G30.9, G31.01, G31.09, G31.1, G31.83, G31.85, G93.44 [Hide] DSM-5: F01.50, F01.511, F01.518, F01.52, F01.53, F01.54, F01.A0, F01.A11, F01.A18, F01.A2, F01.A3, F01.A4, F01.B0, F01.B11, F01.B18, F01.B2, F01.B3, F01.B4, F01.C0, F01.C11, F01.C18, F01.C2, F01.C3, F01.C4, F02.80, F02.811, F02.818, F02.82, F02.83, F02.84, F02.A0, F02.A11, F02.A18, F02.A2, F02.A3, F02.A4, F02.B0, F02.B11, F02.B18, F02.B2, F02.B3, F02.B4, F02.C0, F02.C11, F02.C18, F02.C2, F02.C3, F02.C4, F03.90, F03.911, F03.918, F03.92, F03.93, F03.94, F03.A0, F03.A11, F03.A18, F03.A2, F03.A3, F03.A4, F03.B0, F03.B11, F03.B18, F03.B2, F03.B3, F03.B4, F03.C0, F03.C11, F03.C18, F03.C2, F03.C3, F03.C4, F06.70, F06.71, F10.27, F10.97, F13.27, F13.97, F18.17, F18.27, F18.97, F19.17, F19.27, F19.97 [Hide] MCG Health Chronic Care 28th Edition Copyright  2024 MCG Health, LLC All Rights Reserved Last Update: 3/14/2024 1:53:58 AM Build Number: 28.1.20240313235330.016020 https://careweb.careguidelines.com/ed28/ccg/iccg_250.htm?printview=1 42/42

Patient: How can you care for depression to keep it from getting worse and causing problems? (Check items understood.) QM Adhere to self-care plan Ask for help when feelings are overwhelming Exercise Follow a daily care plan Follow an eating (diet) plan Get all follow-up laboratory tests when due Keep a diary about feelings, activities, eating times, and medicines taken Keep all doctor appointments Keep a vaccination schedule Know when to contact the doctor Manage situation or event triggers Manage stress Seek emergency care for signs of crisis plan not working Set realistic expectations for feeling better, managing symptoms, and recovery Stop using alcohol, tobacco, or drugs Take medicines as ordered Other, specify: _______________________________________ Unknown [P3] Nothing [P3] Not all appropriate information known [P3] Q6. Depression information needed Goal: Depression diagnosis better understood(23)(24) Interventions: ASSIST: Identifying patient's goal priorities, willingness/ability to achieve goals, and preferences for communication and follow-up ASSIST: Goal setting that is specific, measurable, achievable, relevant, and with target completion time (eg, short/long- term, prioritized) ASSIST: Appointment scheduling EDUCATE: Definition: Depression is an alteration in an individual's mood that affects eating, sleeping, and feelings about self and life.(25) It can be expressed by feelings of sadness, hopelessness, and a pessimistic outlook on life. Depression may coexist with and complicate management of other illnesses. Depression may be a sign of other psychiatric conditions (eg, anxiety, phobias, substance misuse).(26) Subsequent episodes common; recurrent episodes increase in intensity, with shorter time frames between episodes.(27) EDUCATE: Depression types: Bipolar depression is a mood disorder with unusual shifts in mood, energy, activity levels, and ability to carry out day-to-day tasks.(12)(26) Major depressive disorder (clinical depression) is a debilitating mood disorder with pervasive and persistent sadness, despair, and a diminished ability to think or make decisions.(12)(27) Postpartum depression occurs within the first 4 weeks to 1 year after delivery; a type of major depression, signs are the same plus inability to care for child or desire to harm child.[E](28) Dysthymia (persistent depressive disorder) is a chronic mildly depressed mood; less severe symptoms than major depression but still affects capacity to function.(27) Seasonal depression (seasonal affective disorder (SAD)) is a combination of biological and mood disturbances affected by the changing seasons; depressed symptoms usually start in winter and subside in spring/summer.(12) EDUCATE: Causes(8): Exact cause is unknown. Family history (genetics) Psychosocial and environmental influences Traumatic event history EDUCATE: Symptoms(27): Concentration difficulty Thought process problems (eg, planning, reasoning, decision making) Appetite changes Emotional difficulties Fatigue Hygiene poor Interest in activities or hobbies lost Sleep problems Social changes (eg, isolation, withdrawal) EDUCATE: Diagnosis: Based on medical history, risk factors, physical examination, and diagnostic tests https://careweb.careguidelines.com/ed28/ccg/iccg_204.htm?printview=1 13/26
