Treatment options(26): Medication management Behavioral health treatment (eg, cognitive behavioral therapy (CBT), individual therapy, family therapy)(29)(52) Bright light therapy(27) Electroconvulsive therapy (ECT) Transcranial magnetic stimulation (TMS)(27)(29) EDUCATE: Check with provider before using natural or herbal treatments. EDUCATE: Bright light therapy information, if appropriate(27)(38): Light therapy is used as a first-line treatment for seasonal affective disorder (SAD). Ocular exposure to intense levels of artificial light (eg, 2500 lux for 2 hours) Symptom improvement may be seen in 1 to 2 weeks. Relapse may occur if treatment is discontinued. Side effects include headache, eyestrain, nausea, irritability, photophobia, and insomnia (when used late in the day).(13) EDUCATE: Electroconvulsive therapy (ECT), if appropriate(53): Used primarily as treatment for severe major depression, catatonia, psychotic depression, or treatment-resistant depression ECT is safe and may be more effective than pharmacotherapy. Maintenance ECT is common. Side effects include temporary memory loss and confusion. Risks include mortality for patient with compromised cardiac status and permanent memory loss. Relapse possible EDUCATE: Transcranial magnetic stimulation (TMS), if appropriate(38)(54): A brief and intense magnetic field within a coil resting on the scalp to painlessly penetrate human tissue and induce cortical neural activation or inhibition It does not require anesthesia, and it has not been associated with adverse memory effects. It is contraindicated with cochlear implants, deep brain stimulators, and vagus nerve stimulators. Use caution with metallic hardware or implanted magnetic-sensitive medical devices (eg, implanted cardioverter- defibrillator, pacemaker, metal aneurysm clips or coils).(25) COORDINATE: Behavioral health services referral, if indicated COORDINATE: Psychiatrist referral, if indicated(26) COORDINATE: Outpatient imaging, tests, or procedures, if indicated(29) COORDINATE: Laboratory testing, if indicated COORDINATE: Care team meeting, if needed(19)(20) COORDINATE: Follow-up contact to review understanding of depression treatment options and need for further assistance(4)(5) https://careweb.careguidelines.com/ed28/ccg/iccg_204.htm?printview=1 16/26

Depression potential complications and comorbidities monitoring needed Goal: Depression potential complication or comorbidity evaluated and addressed(23) Interventions: ASSIST: Identifying patient's goal priorities, willingness/ability to achieve goals, and preferences for communication and follow-up ASSIST: Goal setting that is specific, measurable, achievable, relevant, and with target completion time (eg, short/long- term, prioritized) ASSIST: Appointment scheduling for evaluation of problems, comorbidities, and self-care plan[G]: Recently hospitalized patient's evaluation within 1 week of discharge Recently hospitalized patient's evaluation with outpatient healthcare providers (eg, nurse, social worker) within 1 to 3 days of discharge(4) Appointment every 3 to 6 months when patient is symptom free Appointment once a month or more for patient with questionable adherence to treatment plan EDUCATE: When and how to get help for worsening depression problems EDUCATE: Treatment and monitoring for comorbid conditions (eg, coronary artery disease, high blood pressure, heart valve disease)(27) EDUCATE: Follow-up appointments for assessment of clinical status EDUCATE: Treating depression can help improve outcomes of coexisting chronic medical conditions. EDUCATE: Treatment of coexisting chronic medical conditions may alleviate or eliminate depression.(8) EDUCATE: Medications prescribed for medical or other behavioral health conditions can cause depression(8)(13): Corticosteroids and hormones Interferon Chemotherapeutic agents Levodopa Antipsychotics Sedative: barbiturates, benzodiazepines Analgesics: NSAIDs, opioids H2-receptor antagonists Antihypertensives: beta-blockers, calcium channel blockers EDUCATE: Medications for pre-existing conditions may need to be changed (type or dose). EDUCATE: Certain medications prescribed for depression may worsen chronic medical conditions.(55) EDUCATE: Effects of behavioral health condition and conjunctive medication usage during pregnancy and lactation(28) EDUCATE: Recurrence information(56): Recurrence risks include 2 or more depressive episodes, significant functional impairment, residual symptoms, comorbid chronic health problems. Treatment maintenance to lower risk of relapse and improve outcomes when symptoms subside Antidepressant treatment cessation (under clinician supervision) possible for low-risk patients in remission COORDINATE: Pharmacy medication support, if indicated COORDINATE: Specialist referral, if indicated COORDINATE: Treatment program referral, if indicated(49) COORDINATE: Preconception specialist referral, as needed COORDINATE: Care team meeting, if needed(19)(20) COORDINATE: Communication between care team members and care plan reconciliation(19)(21)(22) COORDINATE: Follow-up contact to review self-care plan and need for further assistance(4)(5) SEND: Be ready for your doctor visit - Depression (Spanish) SEND: Be ready with questions for your doctor - Depression (Spanish) SEND: Narcotic medicine side effects (Spanish) SEND: Pain medicines - Addiction and withdrawal (Spanish) SEND: Be ready for your doctor visit - Substance-related disorders (Spanish) SEND: Be ready with questions for your doctor - Substance-related disorders (Spanish) P6. Depression medication management needed Goal: Medication information understood(57) Interventions: ASSIST: Identifying patient's goal priorities, willingness/ability to achieve goals, and preferences for communication and follow-up ASSIST: Goal setting that is specific, measurable, achievable, relevant, and with target completion time (eg, short/long- term, prioritized) ASSIST: Appointment scheduling with provider for discussion on appropriate medication ASSIST: Providing reconciled medication list to all providers. See Medication Reconciliation Tool SR for more information. EDUCATE: Purpose: Importance of understanding what each medication is for, how it should be taken, and what to do if dose is missed EDUCATE: Medication indications, side effects, and considerations frequently change. See a drug reference for specific information not listed here. EDUCATE: Administration techniques: Route (eg, oral, intramuscular (IM), intravenous (IV)) With or without foods or liquids (eg, milk) Timing of administration Appropriate measuring device (eg, liquid medications) Proper storage EDUCATE: Get emergency help: Adverse or allergic reactions (eg, wheezing, chest tightness, fever, itching, bad cough, blue skin color, hemorrhage, seizures, swelling of face, lips, tongue, or throat) EDUCATE: Side effects: Management, and when to contact provider Common side effects include gastrointestinal changes, dry mouth, fatigue, insomnia, rash, dizziness, and headache. EDUCATE: Considerations: Discuss medication with provider if pregnant, planning a pregnancy, or breast-feeding. Comorbidities may be contraindications to the use of a drug (eg, liver disease, diabetes). Health checks, diary, and action plans (eg, blood pressure, weight, heart rate) Safety measures (eg, sun protection, driving, or operating machinery) Contact provider or pharmacist prior to taking any other over-the-counter or prescribed medication. COORDINATE: Clinical monitoring appropriate to medication use (eg, laboratory tests) COORDINATE: Communication between patient and pharmacy (for obtaining prescriptions, setting up home medication delivery, review for drug-drug interactions) COORDINATE: Communication between care team members and medication list reconciliation COORDINATE: Follow-up to evaluate adherence or problems with medication, provide additional education and support, and identify need for further assistance(4)(5) COORDINATE: Laboratory testing, if indicated COORDINATE: Outpatient imaging, tests, or procedures, if indicated COORDINATE: Therapeutic drug level monitoring SEND: Managing your medicines (Illustrated) (Spanish) P8. Atypical antidepressant medication management needed Goal: Atypical antidepressant medication initiated and maintained(12)(58) Interventions: ASSIST: Appointment scheduling with prescribing provider for medication evaluation and refills EDUCATE: Purpose: Atypical antidepressant medications are used to decrease the frequency and intensity of depressive episodes. EDUCATE: Side effects: Dry mouth, weight changes, insomnia Trazodone or mirtazapine may cause somnolence. Trazodone may cause priapism. EDUCATE: Considerations: Avoid bupropion in patients with seizure disorders. SEND: Antidepressant medicines (Spanish) P9. Atypical antipsychotic medication management needed Goal: Atypical antipsychotic medication initiated and maintained Interventions: ASSIST: Appointment scheduling with prescribing provider for medication evaluation and refills EDUCATE: Purpose: Atypical antipsychotics may be used together with antidepressants to treat major depressive disorder.(27)(59) https://careweb.careguidelines.com/ed28/ccg/iccg_204.htm?printview=1 18/26
10/10/24, 7:06 PM CCG - Depression - Self-Care Second-generation antipsychotic medication EDUCATE: Call provider immediately: Neuroleptic malignant syndrome (NMS) (eg, extreme muscle rigidity, high fever, tachycardia, tachypnea, diaphoresis, stupor, fluctuations in blood pressure)[H](60) EDUCATE: Side effects: Difficulty urinating; jaundice; shuffling walk; fainting spells; seizures; pharyngitis; muscle spasms; excitement or restlessness; jerky movements of head, face, or neck; tachycardia; weight gain; blurred vision(61) Increased risk of metabolic side effects, including obesity, diabetes, and dyslipidemia(62) EDUCATE: Considerations(62): Use cautiously with history of seizures, liver disease, heart disease, blood disorders, or pregnancy.(55) Monitor blood glucose, cholesterol, and triglyceride levels. Talk to provider before discontinuing medication. COORDINATE: Electrocardiogram (ECG) monitoring, if indicated(62) COORDINATE: Laboratory testing P10. Monoamine oxidase inhibitor (MAOI) medication management needed Goal: Monoamine oxidase inhibitor (MAOI) medication initiated and maintained(12)(58) Interventions: ASSIST: Appointment scheduling with prescribing provider for medication evaluation and refills EDUCATE: Purpose: MAOIs are antidepressant medications that decrease the frequency and intensity of depressive episodes.(8) EDUCATE: Call provider immediately: Serotonin syndrome and hypertensive crisis signs: Serotonin syndrome (eg, tachycardia, diarrhea, hyperthermia, tremor, clonus, agitation, delirium) Hypertensive crisis (eg, blood pressure of 180/120 mm Hg or higher; severe headache; motor or sensory deficits; somnolence; seizure; unexplained chest, abdominal, or back pain) EDUCATE: Side effects: Jaundice, hallucinations, fainting, hyperexcitability, confusion, fever, disorientation, decreased sexual function EDUCATE: Considerations: Do not take any new medications (including over-the-counter medications) unless cleared with prescribing provider. Life-threatening drug interactions may occur.[I] Avoid foods with amino acid tyramine, as hypertensive crisis can occur if tyramine consumed with MAOIs. Tyramine is found in foods such as avocados, bananas, yogurt, aged cheese, yeast, and aged meat.(55) Prevent sudden drop in blood pressure and related falls by rising slowly from sitting or lying down. SEND: Antidepressant medicines (Spanish) SEND: How to read food labels (Illustrated) (Spanish) P11. Selective serotonin reuptake inhibitors (SSRIs) or serotonin-norepinephrine reuptake inhibitors (SNRIs) medication management needed Goal: Selective serotonin reuptake inhibitors (SSRIs) or serotonin-norepinephrine reuptake inhibitors (SNRIs) medication initiated and maintained Interventions: ASSIST: Appointment scheduling with prescribing provider for medication evaluation and refills EDUCATE: Purpose: SSRIs and SNRIs are antidepressant medications.(8) Prevent anxiety symptoms, reduce relapse risk, and have less safety risks compared to benzodiazepines(63) Effects may take several weeks.(59) EDUCATE: Call provider immediately: If taking SSRIs and MAOIs or TCAs and serotonin syndrome signs present(64) (65): Serotonin syndrome (eg, tachycardia, diarrhea, hyperthermia, tremor, clonus, agitation, delirium) EDUCATE: Side effects: Agitation, headache, postural hypotension, sexual dysfunction (eg, abnormal ejaculation, impotence, loss of orgasm)(66) SNRIs have fewer sexual side effects than SSRIs. EDUCATE: Considerations(66)(67): Take dose early in the day to help avoid insomnia at night. Use cautiously with cardiac conditions due to possible elevation in blood pressure with SNRIs. SEND: Help with sleeping or insomnia (Spanish) SEND: Antidepressant medicines (Spanish) P12. Social support needed Goal: Social support improved(68)(69) Interventions: ASSIST: Identifying patient's goal priorities, willingness/ability to achieve goals, and preferences for communication and follow-up ASSIST: Goal setting that is specific, measurable, achievable, relevant, and with target completion time (eg, short/long- term, prioritized) ASSIST: Patient in establishment of a helpful social network(70): Identification of individuals in support network Engaging in social support system (gathering information, helping patient develop skills, encouraging shifts in perspective, and allowing patient to vent about issues) EDUCATE: Ways to access social support EDUCATE: Role of social support in quality of life (eg, improved self-image, coping)(69) EDUCATE: Patient's role in social support relationships (eg, be aware of how actions impact the feelings of family and friends)(71) COORDINATE: Behavioral health services referral, if indicated COORDINATE: Community services referral, as needed(72) COORDINATE: Social worker referral, as needed(18) COORDINATE: Support group referral, as needed(73)(74) COORDINATE: Support network contact (eg, church, social groups), as needed(70) COORDINATE: Follow-up to evaluate social support, referral status, and need for further assistance(4)(5) P14. Sleeping problems present Goal: Sleep improved Interventions: ASSIST: Identifying patient's goal priorities, willingness/ability to achieve goals, and preferences for communication and follow-up ASSIST: Goal setting that is specific, measurable, achievable, relevant, and with target completion time (eg, short/long- term, prioritized) ASSIST: Scheduling provider appointment for medical evaluation of sleep changes and creation of self-care plan to include(75)(76): Identifying techniques to improve sleep quality(77) Medication changes that can help patient sleep better (eg, changing the time stimulants are taken to early in the day or not taking diuretic before bedtime) Review of herbal preparations that may promote sleep(78) Creation of sleep diary(79) EDUCATE: Sleep hygiene techniques(75)(77)(80): Schedule sleep (go to bed and get up at same time every day). Spend no more than 8 hours per day in bed. Amount of average sleep time for adult is 7 to 8 hours. Limit napping to once a day for 20 to 30 minutes in early afternoon. Sleep in a dark, quiet bedroom. Use bedroom only for sleeping, not to watch TV, work, use electronics, or do other activities. Avoid pets in the bed. Get safe amount of sunlight every day and avoid bright lights before bedtime. Cut down on drinking fluids in the afternoon if nighttime urination is a problem. Avoid substances that may influence sleep (eg, caffeine, stimulants, alcohol). Stop smoking; nicotine is a stimulant. Get regular exercise, but avoid vigorous exercise 2 hours before bedtime.(81) Write down urgent thoughts if mind-racing is a problem. Do not eat big meals before bedtime; gastric process can cause sleep problems. EDUCATE: Herbal preparations to help sleep (eg, melatonin, tryptophan, valerian), as approved by provider(82) EDUCATE: Behavioral health treatment options and benefits (eg, cognitive behavioral therapy for insomnia)(79)(83) EDUCATE: Hypnotic medications use and misuse, if prescribed for insomnia(76) COORDINATE: Sleep specialist referral, if indicated(82) COORDINATE: Behavioral health services referral, if indicated(79) https://careweb.careguidelines.com/ed28/ccg/iccg_204.htm?printview=1 20/26
10/10/24, 7:06 PM CCG - Depression - Self-Care COORDINATE: Communication between care team members and care plan reconciliation(19)(21)(22) COORDINATE: Follow-up contact to evaluate sleep, status of referrals, and need for further assistance(4)(5) SEND: Help with sleeping or insomnia (Spanish) Evidence Summary Quality standards: The Case Management Society of America's Standards of Practice for Case Management principles include facilitation of informed decision making, counseling, and health education with patient self-care that is responsive to the patient's preferences (eg, culture, values, needs).(84) Quality standards: Standards indicate that program content should include an individualized self-care plan that includes patient preferences, prioritized goals, interventions with information on whom to contact in urgent situations, and scheduled follow-up. Programs should facilitate communication and collaboration with practitioners in a timely manner.(84)(85)(86)(87) National guidelines: The 2018 Physical Activity Guidelines Advisory Committee advises that adults with chronic conditions or disabilities, who are able, should follow the key activity guidelines for adults. The guidelines recommend that adults should do at least 150 to 300 minutes a week of moderate-intensity or 75 to 150 minutes a week of vigorous-intensity aerobic physical activity, or an equivalent combination of moderate-intensity and vigorous-intensity aerobic activity. They should also do muscle-strengthening activities on 2 or more days a week. Older adults should do multicomponent physical activity that includes balance training as well as aerobic and muscle-strengthening activities.(37) Evidence: A randomized controlled study based on 526 patients found that smoking cessation can be achieved among people with severe mental illness without negatively impacting mental health (eg, depression, anxiety).(88) Quality standards: Standards indicate that programs should ensure that during care transitions the care plan is re-evaluated, modified, and communicated to appropriate care team members (eg, providers, caregivers) as needed to support the care transition.(84)(85)(86) (87) Transition consideration: Several studies demonstrated a reduction in 30-day hospital readmissions when the patient follow-up was within 7 days of hospital discharge.(89)(90)(91) Earlier follow-up (eg, within 48 hours after hospital discharge) may also help reduce hospital readmissions for high-risk or vulnerable patients.(4) Follow-up appointment attendance rates were higher when appointments were scheduled before hospital discharge.(92)(93) Outcomes: Several studies support the role of the pharmacist in medication reconciliation to identify and resolve pharmacy-related problems and improve outcomes (eg, prevention of medication-related adverse events, discontinuation of inappropriate medication). (94) Quality standards: Standards indicate that program content should address patient supports and psychosocial issues that may impede self-management. Programs should have a process for following up with patients on the status of referrals.(84) Quality standards: Standards indicate that program content should include a condition treatment plan, self-management and self- monitoring, and health behaviors (eg, sleep patterns). Programs should have a process for following up with patients on the status of referrals.(84) Specialty society: In a systematic review of the pharmacologic treatment of chronic insomnia in adults, the American Academy of Sleep Medicine does not recommend diphenhydramine, melatonin, tryptophan, or valerian for treating sleep onset or maintenance.(78) Specialty society: The American College of Physicians recommends cognitive behavioral therapy for insomnia as the first-line treatment for patients with chronic insomnia (trouble falling or staying asleep for 3 or more nights a week for at least 3 months, with daytime consequences). Cognitive behavioral therapy for insomnia generally includes cognitive therapy with sleep restriction, relaxation training, and stimulus control for 5 to 8 sessions.(95) References 1. [ Context Link 1 ] Footnotes [A] Assess and address the emergency or urgent problems before continuing the engagement. [ A in Context Link 1 ] [B] A self-care plan tells you how to take care of your condition and know when and where to get help for problems. [ B in Context Link 1 ] [C] Patients with persistent thoughts of suicide, homicide, or serious harm to self or another may need frequent monitoring for progression to planning or intention to act on their thoughts. Inpatient treatment may be appropriate for people with depression who are at significant risk of suicide, self-harm, or self-neglect.(2) Other patients who will reliably participate in monitoring and have supports and providers available may be safely treated with frequent monitoring in outpatient care.(6)(9) [ C in Context Link 1 ] [D] Restricting access to suicide methods includes removing firearms or other weapons from the home, keeping only immediate medication dosages available, removing materials that could be used for hanging or suffocation, and access restrictions for jumping (eg, closed-caption video monitoring, physical barrier to bridges).(11)(12)(39)(96) [ D in Context Link 1 ] [E] Two other depressive disorders specific to the postpartum period are mild postpartum blues and severe puerperal psychosis.(28) [ E in Context Link 1 ] [F] Telehealth may improve access to and coordination of mental health care. Patients with behavioral health diagnoses may use telehealth or telepsychiatry to access mental health support or care (eg, to get assistance with problem-solving techniques or ask about presenting symptoms or medication use).(97) [ F in Context Link 1 ] [G] The timing of provider follow-up depends on the patient's disease severity, self-care complexity, patient/caregiver willingness and ability to provide patient care, and available social supports.(98) [ G in Context Link 1 ] [H] Neuroleptic malignant syndrome is a rare, rapidly progressive, potentially fatal complication more common with typical antipsychotics. Onset can occur hours or years after initiation of drug therapy.(60) [ H in Context Link 1 ] [I] Medications to avoid include sympathomimetics (eg, amphetamines, combination cold tablets, nasal decongestants, and appetite suppressants), illicit drugs, narcotic medications (eg, meperidine, tramadol, and methadone), selective serotonin reuptake inhibitors (SSRIs), and tricyclic antidepressants (TCAs).(66) [ I in Context Link 1 ] Codes ICD-10 Diagnosis: F06.30, F06.31, F06.32, F06.34, F10.14, F10.24, F10.94, F11.14, F11.24, F11.94, F13.14, F13.24, F13.94, F14.14, F14.24, F14.94, F15.14, F15.24, F15.94, F16.14, F16.24, F16.94, F18.14, F18.24, F18.94, F19.14, F19.24, F19.94, F32.0, F32.1, F32.2, F32.3, F32.4, F32.81, F32.89, F32.9, F32.A, F33.0, F33.1, F33.2, F33.3, F33.40, F33.41, F33.9, F34.0, F34.1, F34.89, F43.21, F43.23, F53.0, F53.1, O90.6, R45.88, Z13.31, Z13.32, Z91.51, Z91.52 [Hide] DSM-5: F06.31, F06.32, F06.34, F10.14, F10.24, F10.94, F11.14, F11.24, F11.94, F13.14, F13.24, F13.94, F14.14, F14.24, F14.94, F15.14, F15.24, F15.94, F16.14, F16.24, F16.94, F18.14, F18.24, F18.94, F19.14, F19.24, F19.94, F32.0, F32.1, F32.2, F32.3, F32.4, F32.81, F32.89, F32.9, F32.A, F33.0, F33.1, F33.2, F33.3, F33.41, F33.9, F34.0, F34.1, F43.21, F43.23, R45.88 [Hide] MCG Health Chronic Care 28th Edition https://careweb.careguidelines.com/ed28/ccg/iccg_204.htm?printview=1 25/26

Plan of Care Problems P1. Schizophrenia information needed Goal: Schizophrenia diagnosis better understood(4)(5) Interventions: ASSIST: Identifying patient's goal priorities, willingness/ability to achieve goals, and preferences for communication and follow-up ASSIST: Goal setting that is specific, measurable, achievable, relevant, and with target completion time (eg, short/long- term, prioritized) ASSIST: Appointment scheduling EDUCATE: Definition(6)(7): Complex brain disease that results in impaired thoughts, perceptions, cognitive functions, moods, and motivations Often called a psychotic disorder because individuals lose touch with reality and exhibit psychotic symptoms (eg, hallucinations, delusions, or catatonia) Causes profound disruption in lives of those diagnosed and the people around them Lifelong debilitating illness with no cure EDUCATE: Causes(6)(7): Exact cause is unknown. Family history (genetics) Traumatic event history Biochemical influences (eg, natural levels of dopamine, serotonin, and norepinephrine) Brain structure and cellular changes EDUCATE: Symptoms(8): Cognitive changes (eg, confusion, memory loss) Delusions (eg, paranoid) Emotional affect alteration (eg, flat, inappropriate, blunt, labile) Social changes (eg, isolation, withdrawal) Agitation Hallucinations Thought process problems (eg, disorganized speech, trouble completing activities, concentration difficulty) Sleep problems EDUCATE: Diagnosis(9)(10): Based on medical history, risk factors, physical examination, and diagnostic tests Behavioral health examination and history EDUCATE: Treatment: ADL and IADL support Behavioral health therapy options Comorbid condition management(11) Medication management No cure Psychotherapy (eg, cognitive behavioral therapy (CBT), individual or group therapy, family therapy) Self-care activities (eg, diet, activity, medication) Symptom management(12) EDUCATE: Treatment goals: Complication and progression management Educate and support caregivers Psychosocial functioning improved and maintained Quality of life improvement Recurrence prevention Relapse reduction(12)(13) Remission(10) Safety (for self and others) Symptom frequency and severity reduced Functional improvement and maintenance (eg, ADL, IADL) EDUCATE: Resources: National Alliance on Mental Illness: www.nami.org National Institute of Mental Health: www.nimh.nih.gov National Suicide Prevention Lifeline: www.988lifeline.org https://careweb.careguidelines.com/ed28/ccg/iccg_203.htm?printview=1 14/29

Schizophrenia self-care needed Goal: Schizophrenia self-care plan developed, implemented, and maintained(4)(21)(22) Interventions: ASSIST: Identifying patient's goal priorities, willingness/ability to achieve goals, and preferences for communication and follow-up ASSIST: Goal setting that is specific, measurable, achievable, relevant, and with target completion time (eg, short/long- term, prioritized) ASSIST: Support system (eg, family, friends) identification, enlistment, and education ASSIST: Medical alert bracelet/necklace, obtain and wear ASSIST: Appointment scheduling ASSIST: Self-care plan creation between patient and provider (eg, goals, management, treating symptoms, getting help) ASSIST: Communication between multiple providers(23) EDUCATE: Self-care plan information(12): Action plan (eg, when to initiate relaxation, cognitive, and stress-management techniques) Adherence to plan necessary to achieve goals, and complications of nonadherence (eg, exacerbations, progression, comorbidities)[C](8) Auditory hallucination care techniques (eg, use headphones with music, talk to others) Behavioral health treatment (eg, cognitive behavioral therapy (CBT), individual therapy, family therapy)[D](6)(9) Caregiver support plan, as needed Crisis care plan: turn off distractions (eg, TV, radio), seek emergent care, call hotline, call police if escalates; caregivers advised to not argue(6)(24) Daily care (eg, keep a routine, good hygiene, take medicine) Diet considerations (eg, limit caffeine, avoid alcohol)(9)(25) Exercise program within provider's recommendations(26) Functional changes care (eg, adaptations and support for ADL and IADL) Health checks, diary, and action plans (eg, write down thoughts, feelings, daily activities, eating times, and when medication taken)(6) Medication purpose, use, effects, and considerations (eg, timing before meals or exercise)(27) Relapse signs (eg, insomnia, decreased appetite, poor personal hygiene)(8) Safety awareness and management (eg, remove suicide risks, de-escalation techniques) Self-care plan guided adjustment, if indicated Set small goals every day (eg, get out of bed at a certain time, finish one chore or project, or do something that brings pleasure like listen to music).(6)(12) Sleep hygiene (eg, regular pattern, calm sleep environment) Substance use avoidance[E](6)(9) Support group participation Tobacco cessation plan(27) Trigger avoidance (eg, high-stress situations, sleep deprivation)(8) Wellness checkups (eg, vaccinations, screenings) EDUCATE: Caregiver interventions to support patient self-care(28): Avoid confrontation unless necessary to prevent harmful behavior.(7)(8) https://careweb.careguidelines.com/ed28/ccg/iccg_203.htm?printview=1 15/29
10/10/24, 7:06 PM CCG - Schizophrenia - Self-Care Delusion and hallucination management techniques (eg, do not disagree or argue, maintain reality, use distractions, ask about hallucination)(6) Directions should be specific and concrete; avoid relying on nonverbal communication. Learn about disorder, symptoms, precipitating factors, and comorbidities. Orient, support, and assist patient as needed.(8) Problem-solving techniques (eg, negotiation)(11) Promote personal self-care as much as possible; praise successes.(8) Set limits; provide structure and safe environment. Sleeping problems present Goal: Sleep improved Interventions: ASSIST: Identifying patient's goal priorities, willingness/ability to achieve goals, and preferences for communication and follow-up ASSIST: Goal setting that is specific, measurable, achievable, relevant, and with target completion time (eg, short/long- term, prioritized) ASSIST: Scheduling provider appointment for medical evaluation of sleep changes and creation of self-care plan to include(44)(45): Identifying techniques to improve sleep quality(46) Medication changes that can help patient sleep better (eg, changing the time stimulants are taken to early in the day or not taking diuretic before bedtime) Review of herbal preparations that may promote sleep(47) Creation of sleep diary(48) EDUCATE: Sleep hygiene techniques(44)(46)(49): Schedule sleep (go to bed and get up at same time every day). Spend no more than 8 hours per day in bed. Amount of average sleep time for adult is 7 to 8 hours. Limit napping to once a day for 20 to 30 minutes in early afternoon. Sleep in a dark, quiet bedroom. Use bedroom only for sleeping, not to watch TV, work, use electronics, or do other activities. Avoid pets in the bed. Get safe amount of sunlight every day and avoid bright lights before bedtime. Cut down on drinking fluids in the afternoon if nighttime urination is a problem. Avoid substances that may influence sleep (eg, caffeine, stimulants, alcohol). Stop smoking; nicotine is a stimulant. Get regular exercise, but avoid vigorous exercise 2 hours before bedtime.(50) Write down urgent thoughts if mind-racing is a problem. Do not eat big meals before bedtime; gastric process can cause sleep problems. EDUCATE: Herbal preparations to help sleep (eg, melatonin, tryptophan, valerian), as approved by provider(51) EDUCATE: Behavioral health treatment options and benefits (eg, cognitive behavioral therapy for insomnia)(48)(52) EDUCATE: Hypnotic medications use and misuse, if prescribed for insomnia(45) COORDINATE: Sleep specialist referral, if indicated(51) COORDINATE: Behavioral health services referral, if indicated(48) COORDINATE: Communication between care team members and care plan reconciliation(15)(17)(18) COORDINATE: Follow-up contact to evaluate sleep, status of referrals, and need for further assistance(19)(20) SEND: Help with sleeping or insomnia (Spanish) P9. Vaccinations not up to date, increasing infection risk Goal: Vaccinations up to date(75)(76) Interventions: ASSIST: Identifying patient's goal priorities, willingness/ability to achieve goals, and preferences for communication and follow-up ASSIST: Goal setting that is specific, measurable, achievable, relevant, and with target completion time (eg, short/long- term, prioritized) ASSIST: Appointment scheduling ASSIST: Identifying vaccines patient may need that are not contraindicated based on clinical status EDUCATE: Purpose and benefits of vaccinations EDUCATE: Recommended immunizations and timing of administration: Centers for Disease Control and Prevention's vaccination schedule: www.cdc.gov/vaccines(77) Vaccine schedules may need to be altered based on medications; check with the specialist provider for information.(78) Possible side effects or complications associated with getting vaccinated EDUCATE: Influenza vaccination annually(79): Side effects are mild and last 1 or 2 days. Common side effects with shot are sore, red, or swollen area where shot was given; fever; and aches. Common side effects with nasal spray are mild and last 1 or 2 days; they include runny nose, headache, sore throat, and cough. Allergic reaction rare and is usually caused by a chicken egg allergy EDUCATE: Pneumococcal vaccination potential side effects: Side effects are mild and last 1 or 2 days. Common side effects include a sore, red, or swollen area where shot was given; fever; and muscle aches. Serious side effects are rare. EDUCATE: COVID-19 vaccination potential side effects(77): Side effects are mild and last 1 or 2 days. Common side effects include a sore, red, or swollen area where shot was given; chills; fatigue; and aches. Serious side effects are rare. (13) Evidence: A meta-analysis reported that only 13.5% of patients met strict criteria for recovery, which included the presence of at most mild symptoms and good social functioning, with at least one of these outcomes lasting at least 2 years.(9) Quality standards: Standards indicate that program content should include an individualized self-care plan that includes patient preferences, prioritized goals, interventions with information on whom to contact in urgent situations, and scheduled follow-up. Programs should facilitate communication and collaboration with practitioners in a timely manner.(80)(81)(82)(83) National guidelines: The National Institute for Health and Care Excellence (NICE) guidelines suggest that all patients (adults and children) with psychosis or schizophrenia, including those with acute or recurrent symptoms, receive family therapy (if applicable) as an adjunct to individual cognitive behavioral therapy (CBT).(27) https://careweb.careguidelines.com/ed28/ccg/iccg_203.htm?printview=1 23/29
10/10/24, 7:06 PM CCG - Schizophrenia - Self-Care Evidence: Randomized controlled trials and meta-analyses have found that when psychosocial care (eg, family-based intervention) is integrated with standard pharmacologic care, there is a reduced risk of relapse and hospitalization.(27)(84) Evidence: A review of 24 studies involving 1900 participants suggests that group cognitive behavioral therapy (CBT) may be better than standard care or other psychosocial interventions for overall mental state and global functioning for people with schizophrenia.(85) National guidelines: The 2018 Physical Activity Guidelines Advisory Committee advises that adults with chronic conditions or disabilities, who are able, should follow the key activity guidelines for adults. The guidelines recommend that adults should do at least 150 to 300 minutes a week of moderate-intensity or 75 to 150 minutes a week of vigorous-intensity aerobic physical activity, or an equivalent combination of moderate-intensity and vigorous-intensity aerobic activity. They should also do muscle-strengthening activities on 2 or more days a week. Older adults should do multicomponent physical activity that includes balance training as well as aerobic and muscle-strengthening activities.(86) Specialty society: The National Institute for Health and Care Excellence (NICE) recommends offering combined healthy eating and physical activity programs to help reduce the increased rates of obesity and type 2 diabetes found in adults with psychosis or schizophrenia.(11) Evidence: Studies suggest that having ever used cannabis was associated with a modest increased risk of psychotic disorders, daily use was associated with a higher risk, and daily use of high-potency cannabis conferred a risk of more than 4 times that of ever having used cannabis.(9) Specialty society: The American Psychiatric Association states that smoking is a major contributor to increased mortality in individuals with serious mental illness and advises that health education and motivational interviewing may be helpful in those who have had prior unsuccessful attempts at smoking cessation.(87) Specialty society: Smoking tobacco or marijuana reduces the serum levels of several antipsychotic drugs, including clozapine and olanzapine.(87) Quality standards: Standards indicate that programs should ensure that during care transitions the care plan is re-evaluated, modified, and communicated to appropriate care team members (eg, providers, caregivers) as needed to support the care transition.(80)(81)(82) (83) Transition consideration: Several studies demonstrated a reduction in 30-day hospital readmissions when the patient follow-up was within 7 days of hospital discharge.(88)(89)(90) Earlier follow-up (eg, within 48 hours after hospital discharge) may also help reduce hospital readmissions for high-risk or vulnerable patients.(19) Follow-up appointment attendance rates were higher when appointments were scheduled before hospital discharge.(91)(92) National guidelines: The National Institute for Health and Care Excellence (NICE) guidelines recommend the administration of antipsychotics to treat both acute and recurrent symptoms in schizophrenia.(27) Outcomes: Several studies support the role of the pharmacist in medication reconciliation to identify and resolve pharmacy-related problems and improve outcomes (eg, prevention of medication-related adverse events, discontinuation of inappropriate medication). (93) Quality standards: Standards indicate that program content should include a condition treatment plan, self-management and self- monitoring, and health behaviors (eg, sleep patterns). Programs should have a process for following up with patients on the status of referrals.(80) Specialty society: In a systematic review of the pharmacologic treatment of chronic insomnia in adults, the American Academy of Sleep Medicine does not recommend diphenhydramine, melatonin, tryptophan, or valerian for treating sleep onset or maintenance.(47) Specialty society: The American College of Physicians recommends cognitive behavioral therapy for insomnia as the first-line treatment for patients with chronic insomnia (trouble falling or staying asleep for 3 or more nights a week for at least 3 months, with daytime consequences). Cognitive behavioral therapy for insomnia generally includes cognitive therapy with sleep restriction, relaxation training, and stimulus control for 5 to 8 sessions.(94) Evidence: Multiple studies indicate that the large number and types of medications consumed by the elderly increase the risk of falls and should be evaluated.(44)(64)(95) National guidelines: The U.S. Preventive Services Task Force recommends exercise-based interventions to help reduce falls for people 65 years and older. Other interventions (eg, behavioral therapy, nutrition therapy, environmental modification, medication changes) may be considered based on the patient's preferences, the benefit-to-harm balance, and the circumstances of prior falls. Vitamin D supplementation is not recommended for fall prevention for older people without a known deficiency or osteoporosis.(55) National guidelines: The Institute of Medicine does not specify how much total fluid should come from water. Their recommendation is based on the median of total water intake on dietary surveys, which include water from liquids and solids. For healthy people, the https://careweb.careguidelines.com/ed28/ccg/iccg_203.htm?printview=1 24/29
10/10/24, 7:06 PM CCG - Schizophrenia - Self-Care advice is to consume at least 8 glasses (8 ounces (240 mL) each) of water daily; there is little evidence to support this recommendation. [ B in Context Link 1 ] [C] Adherence in the early stages of the disease has an opportunity to influence its chronic and progressive course.(6) Early intervention in psychosis services can improve clinical outcomes, including admission rates, symptoms, and relapse rates, for people with a first episode of psychosis.(27) [ C in Context Link 1 ] [D] Electroconvulsive therapy (ECT) may be useful for psychotic symptoms, particularly if the patient is not responsive to pharmacologic treatment.(97) [ D in Context Link 1 ] [E] Cocaine, marijuana, and amphetamines can provoke psychotic symptoms in patients with schizophrenia.(8) [ E in Context Link 1 ] [F] Telehealth may improve access to and coordination of mental health care. Patients with behavioral health diagnoses may use telehealth or telepsychiatry to access mental health support or care (eg, to get assistance with problem-solving techniques or ask about presenting symptoms or medication use).(98) [ F in Context Link 1 ] [G] The timing of provider follow-up depends on the patient's disease severity, self-care complexity, patient/caregiver willingness and ability to provide patient care, and available social supports.(99) [ G in Context Link 1 ] [H] Neuroleptic malignant syndrome is a rare, rapidly progressive, potentially fatal complication more common with typical antipsychotics. Onset can occur hours or years after initiation of drug therapy.(41) [ H in Context Link 1, 2 ] https://careweb.careguidelines.com/ed28/ccg/iccg_203.htm?printview=1 28/29
10/10/24, 7:06 PM CCG - Schizophrenia - Self-Care [I] Tardive dyskinesia (TD) is more common with first-generation (typical) antipsychotics. The risk of TD is higher with long-term use of antipsychotics. The offending medication should be removed with the first sign of symptoms, as symptoms may be irreversible.(42) [ I in Context Link 1 ] [J] Use typical antipsychotic medication when psychotic symptoms outweigh the adverse effects of medication.(43) [ J in Context Link 1 ] [K] Fall risk factors include mobility or functional impairment, poor health, weakness, incontinence, polypharmacy (4 or more medications), medication dose changes, or patient taking substances that increase risk (eg, benzodiazepines, sedative-hypnotics, antidepressants, antipsychotics, alcohol, opiates).(64)(100) [ K in Context Link 1 ] [L] A pharmacist can support medication reconciliation for redundancy or duplication and identify drug interactions (eg, with other drugs, food).(61) [ L in Context Link 1 ] [M] Services to consider for community support include stairway modifications, widening of interior doors for ambulation equipment, and installation of handrails.(53) [ M in Context Link 1 ] [N] Adequate intake is used when the recommended daily allowance cannot be determined because of insufficient data on requirements.(69) [ N in Context Link 1 ] Codes ICD-10 Diagnosis: F20.0, F20.1, F20.2, F20.3, F20.5, F20.81, F20.89, F20.9, F25.0, F25.1, F25.8, F25.9 DSM-5: F20.81, F20.9, F25.0, F25.1 MCG Health Chronic Care 28th Edition Copyright  2024 MCG Health, LLC All Rights Reserved Last Update: 3/14/2024 1:47:40 AM Build Number: 28.1.20240313235330.016020 https://careweb.careguidelines.com/ed28/ccg/iccg_203.htm?printview=1 29/29

Personal harm risk, immediate B2. Inadequate social support Problems P1. Substance-related disorders information needed Goal: Substance-related disorders diagnosis better understood(3)(4) Interventions: ASSIST: Identifying patient's goal priorities, willingness/ability to achieve goals, and preferences for communication and follow-up ASSIST: Goal setting that is specific, measurable, achievable, relevant, and with target completion time (eg, short/long- term, prioritized) ASSIST: Appointment scheduling EDUCATE: Definition(5): Disorders of intoxication, dependence, and misuse caused by various legal and illegal substances Neurobiological aspect: Intoxicant exposure increases firing in the dopaminergic pathways of the ventral striatum, which gives a subjective high, promoting further drug use.(6)(7) Chronic disorders may alter the area of the brain that affects judgment and behavior, driving compulsion to obtain and use substances, making abstinence difficult. EDUCATE: Causes: Family history (genetics) Traumatic event history Psychosocial and environmental influences Medications prescribed for conditions (eg, opioids, sleep aids, stimulants) can lead to substance-related disorders. EDUCATE: Symptoms(5): Behavioral changes (eg, sudden mood swings, aggression, isolation) Risky behaviors (eg, gambling, shopping sprees, promiscuity)(8) Excessive time using substances Tolerance to substance Memory impairment (blackouts) Tremors Repeated episodes of intoxication EDUCATE: Diagnosis: Based on medical history, risk factors, physical examination, and diagnostic tests Positive screening instrument EDUCATE: Treatment(8)(9): Psychotherapy (eg, cognitive behavioral therapy (CBT), individual or group therapy, family therapy) Medication management[D] Medications for pre-existing conditions may need to be changed (type or dose). Comorbid condition management Lifestyle changes (eg, nutrition, exercise, tobacco cessation) Symptom management EDUCATE: Treatment goals: Psychosocial functioning improved and maintained Quality of life improvement Relapse reduction EDUCATE: Resources: Al-Anon Family Groups: www.al-anon.org Alcoholics Anonymous World Services: www.aa.org Cocaine Anonymous World Services: www.ca.org Marijuana Anonymous World Services: www.marijuana-anonymous.org Narcotics Anonymous World Services: www.na.org https://careweb.careguidelines.com/ed28/ccg/iccg_259.htm?printview=1 18/42

EDUCATE: Withdrawal treatments may include medication and supportive care (eg, nutrition). EDUCATE: Alcohol withdrawal symptoms and related management: See Alcohol Misuse CCG for more information. EDUCATE: Anabolic steroid withdrawal through drug tapering. Side effects and related management: Side effects of steroid withdrawal include drug craving, fatigue, anorexia, headaches, muscle aches, insomnia, reduced sex drive, mood swings, restlessness, depression, and suicidal ideation. Medications may be prescribed for withdrawal symptoms (eg, antidepressants for depression, analgesics for pain). EDUCATE: Benzodiazepines withdrawal symptoms begin 1 to 5 days after last dose and include anxiety, light or sound sensitivity, confusion, and seizures.(8) EDUCATE: Hallucinogen and inhalant cessation does not cause withdrawal syndrome.(8) EDUCATE: Cannibis withdrawal symptoms (eg, irritability, anxiety, insomnia, appetite disturbance, depression) and related management(21) EDUCATE: Sedative-hypnotics withdrawal symptoms and related management(8): Minor withdrawal symptoms include anxiety, insomnia, tremors, and nightmares. Major withdrawal symptoms include grand mal seizures, psychosis, hyperreflexia, and death. EDUCATE: Nicotine withdrawal symptoms (eg, weight gain, insomnia, irritability, anxiety, depression, restlessness, difficulty concentrating) and related management(8) EDUCATE: Opioid withdrawal symptoms and related management: See Narcotic/Opioid Misuse Assessment CCG for more information. EDUCATE: Stimulant (eg, cocaine, methamphetamine) withdrawal symptoms and related management(8): Stimulant withdrawal symptoms include drug craving, agitation and restlessness, fatigue, unpleasant dreams, slowed activity, and delayed depression. Stimulant withdrawal impacts sleep quality and pattern; sleep hygiene techniques include routine sleep/wake times and sleep in dark room. No medications approved by the US Food and Drug Administration for cocaine relapse prevention(22) COORDINATE: Treatment program referral, if indicated(5) COORDINATE: Follow-up contact to review understanding of substance-related disorder treatment options, referral status, and need for further assistance(19)(20) P2. Comorbidities may be contraindications to the use of a drug (eg, liver disease, diabetes). Health checks, diary, and action plans (eg, blood pressure, weight, heart rate) Safety measures (eg, sun protection, driving, or operating machinery) Contact provider or pharmacist prior to taking any other over-the-counter or prescribed medication. Monitor closely for suicidal ideation and adverse effects, particularly early in treatment. Discontinue medications under provider supervision only, as withdrawal symptoms may occur. Continue taking medication even if feeling better. Potential hazardous or at-risk alcohol use present Goal: Alcohol effects and complications of misuse understood(62) Interventions: ASSIST: Identifying patient's goal priorities, willingness/ability to achieve goals, and preferences for communication and follow-up ASSIST: Goal setting that is specific, measurable, achievable, relevant, and with target completion time (eg, short/long- term, prioritized) ASSIST: Appointment scheduling for evaluation of effects of alcohol use and self-care plan, if needed EDUCATE: Health effects of drinking(62)(78): Light to moderate intake (2 drinks per day for men and 1 drink per day for women) may be linked with poor health outcomes.[C](79)(80) Heavy drinking can cause acute alcohol poisoning and nutritional deficiencies. Long-term effects of alcohol misuse include cirrhosis, pancreatitis, peripheral neuropathy, and cancer.(34) EDUCATE: Follow drinking limits set by provider. EDUCATE: Comparison of personal drinking to national norms(78): Safe drinking levels for men (younger than 65 years) are 14 drinks or fewer per week and 4 drinks or fewer on one occasion. Safe drinking levels for women are 7 drinks or fewer per week and 3 drinks or fewer on one occasion. Safe drinking levels for healthy adults age 65 years are 7 drinks or fewer per week and 3 drinks or fewer on one occasion. No drinking during pregnancy is safe. Caution should be used when breast-feeding; consumption of a single drink may be safe if the mother waits 4 hours before breast-feeding or expresses breast milk for feeding prior to consuming the alcoholic drink. Varenicline can be used as maintenance therapy to prevent relapse.(106) EDUCATE: Call provider immediately: Suicidal ideation, behavioral changes, or seizures(107) EDUCATE: Side effects: Insomnia, tachycardia EDUCATE: Considerations: Avoid bupropion in patients with seizure disorders.(58) SEND: How to stop smoking (Spanish) Goal: Nicotine replacement therapy (NRT) initiated and maintained(58) Interventions: ASSIST: Appointment scheduling with prescribing provider for medication evaluation and refills EDUCATE: Purpose: NRT is used to reduce withdrawal feeling during smoking cessation. Dosage is individualized based on need and response of patient. If using patch, apply one new patch every 24 hours on skin that is dry, clean, and hairless. Rotate skin sites. Do not cut patch, and do not use more than one patch at a time. If using lozenge or gum, do not eat or drink for 15 minutes before or while utilizing. Tobacco use in patient history Goal: Tobacco abstinence maintained(34)(108) Interventions: ASSIST: Identifying patient's goal priorities, willingness/ability to achieve goals, and preferences for communication and follow-up ASSIST: Goal setting that is specific, measurable, achievable, relevant, and with target completion time (eg, short/long- term, prioritized) ASSIST: Appointment scheduling with provider for evaluation of improved or stabilized health status, for reinforcement of abstinence from tobacco EDUCATE: How to keep environment tobacco free EDUCATE: Encourage continued abstinence from smoking and review tobacco cessation strategies. EDUCATE: Resources: American Lung Association: www.lung.org Centers for Disease Control and Prevention: www.cdc.gov/tobacco Smokefree. National Institutes of Health: www.smokefree.gov COORDINATE: Tobacco use treatment, as needed COORDINATE: Support group referral, as needed COORDINATE: Communication between care team members and care plan reconciliation(15)(17)(18) COORDINATE: Follow-up contact to evaluate tobacco use, referral status, and need for further assistance(19)(20) P18. Vaccinations not up to date, increasing infection risk Goal: Vaccinations up to date(109)(110) Interventions: ASSIST: Identifying patient's goal priorities, willingness/ability to achieve goals, and preferences for communication and follow-up ASSIST: Goal setting that is specific, measurable, achievable, relevant, and with target completion time (eg, short/long- term, prioritized) ASSIST: Appointment scheduling ASSIST: Identifying vaccines patient may need that are not contraindicated based on clinical status EDUCATE: Purpose and benefits of vaccinations EDUCATE: Recommended immunizations and timing of administration: Centers for Disease Control and Prevention's vaccination schedule: www.cdc.gov/vaccines(111) Vaccine schedules may need to be altered based on medications; check with the specialist provider for information.(112) Possible side effects or complications associated with getting vaccinated EDUCATE: Influenza vaccination annually(113): Side effects are mild and last 1 or 2 days. Common side effects with shot are sore, red, or swollen area where shot was given; fever; and aches. Common side effects with nasal spray are mild and last 1 or 2 days; they include runny nose, headache, sore throat, and cough. Allergic reaction rare and is usually caused by a chicken egg allergy EDUCATE: Pneumococcal vaccination potential side effects: Side effects are mild and last 1 or 2 days. Common side effects include a sore, red, or swollen area where shot was given; fever; and muscle aches. Serious side effects are rare. EDUCATE: COVID-19 vaccination potential side effects(111): Side effects are mild and last 1 or 2 days. Common side effects include a sore, red, or swollen area where shot was given; chills; fatigue; and aches. Serious side effects are rare. COORDINATE: Community services referral, as needed COORDINATE: Vaccinations COORDINATE: Follow-up to evaluate vaccination understanding, referral status, and need for further assistance(19)(20) SEND: Coronavirus disease 2019 (COVID-19) vaccines (Spanish) SEND: Flu vaccine (Spanish) SEND: Pneumonia vaccine for adults (Spanish) https://careweb.careguidelines.com/ed28/ccg/iccg_259.htm?printview=1 33/42
10/10/24, 7:07 PM CCG - Substance-Related Disorders - Self-Care SEND: Hepatitis A vaccine (Spanish) SEND: Hepatitis B vaccine (Spanish) Evidence Summary Quality standards: The Case Management Society of America's Standards of Practice for Case Management principles include facilitation of informed decision making, counseling, and health education with patient self-care that is responsive to the patient's preferences (eg, culture, values, needs).(114) Quality standards: Standards indicate that program content should include an individualized self-care plan that includes patient preferences, prioritized goals, interventions with information on whom to contact in urgent situations, and scheduled follow-up. Programs should facilitate communication and collaboration with practitioners in a timely manner.(114)(115)(116)(117) National guidelines: The 2018 Physical Activity Guidelines Advisory Committee advises that adults with chronic conditions or disabilities, who are able, should follow the key activity guidelines for adults. The guidelines recommend that adults should do at least 150 to 300 minutes a week of moderate-intensity or 75 to 150 minutes a week of vigorous-intensity aerobic physical activity, or an equivalent combination of moderate-intensity and vigorous-intensity aerobic activity. They should also do muscle-strengthening activities on 2 or more days a week. Older adults should do multicomponent physical activity that includes balance training as well as aerobic and muscle-strengthening activities.(118) Quality standards: Standards indicate that programs should ensure that during care transitions the care plan is re-evaluated, modified, and communicated to appropriate care team members (eg, providers, caregivers) as needed to support the care transition.(114)(115) (116)(117) Transition consideration: Several studies demonstrated a reduction in 30-day hospital readmissions when the patient follow-up was within 7 days of hospital discharge.(119)(120)(121) Earlier follow-up (eg, within 48 hours after hospital discharge) may also help reduce hospital readmissions for high-risk or vulnerable patients.(19) Follow-up appointment attendance rates were higher when appointments were scheduled before hospital discharge.(122)(123) Outcomes: Several studies support the role of the pharmacist in medication reconciliation to identify and resolve pharmacy-related problems and improve outcomes (eg, prevention of medication-related adverse events, discontinuation of inappropriate medication). (124) Evidence: Meta-analysis of alcohol dependence in adults has found that acamprosate may curtail alcohol use significantly when prescribed for durations of 3 to 12 months.(75) Evidence: In a randomized clinical trial, gabapentin was found to be effective in treating alcohol dependence and relapse-related symptoms of insomnia, anxiety, dysphoria, cravings, headaches, and/or pain.(8) Evidence: In a randomized controlled trial, patients who started taking gabapentin after 3 days of abstinence had fewer heavy drinking days (defined as 5 or more drinks for men and 4 or more drinks for women) and greater rates of abstinence than those who received a placebo.(125) Evidence: A systematic review of 8 randomized controlled trials involving 709 participants found that maintenance treatment with buprenorphine appeared to be more effective than nonopioid treatment.(126) National guidelines: The U.S. Preventive Services Task Force recommends screening for depression in patients 19 years and older combined with adequate systems to ensure treatment and follow-up; the task force concludes that such screening improves clinical outcomes (eg, reduction in depressive symptoms).(127) Quality standards: Standards indicate that program content should address psychosocial issues (eg, depression) that may impede self-management. Programs should have a process for following up with patients on the status of referrals.(114) Evidence: The Alcohol Use Disorders Identification Test (AUDIT) questionnaire's 3-item subset of questions about alcohol consumption (AUDIT-C) is a brief screening test for problem drinking that has been validated in primary care office settings.(78) Quality standards: Standards indicate that program content should include a condition treatment plan, self-management and self- monitoring, and health behaviors (eg, substance use). Programs should have a process for following up with patients on the status of referrals.(114) National guidelines: The US Department of Health and Human Services' Dietary Guidelines for Americans suggests that moderate alcohol consumption (2 drinks/day in men or 1 drink/day in women) may increase the overall risk of death from various causes, such as several types of cancer and some forms of cardiovascular disease.(91) https://careweb.careguidelines.com/ed28/ccg/iccg_259.htm?printview=1 34/42
10/10/24, 7:07 PM CCG - Substance-Related Disorders - Self-Care Specialty society: The American Heart Association does not support initiation of alcohol intake to improve cardiovascular health.(79) Quality standards: Standards indicate that program content should address patient supports and psychosocial issues that may impede self-management. Programs should have a process for following up with patients on the status of referrals.(114) National guidelines: The Institute of Medicine does not specify how much total fluid should come from water. Their recommendation is based on the median of total water intake on dietary surveys, which include water from liquids and solids. For healthy people, the advice is to consume at least 8 glasses (8 ounces (240 mL) each) of water daily; there is little evidence to support this recommendation. (128) National guidelines: The U.S. Preventive Services Task Force (USPSTF) recommends that clinicians ask all adults about tobacco use, advise them to stop using tobacco, and provide behavioral interventions and FDA-approved pharmacotherapy for cessation to nonpregnant adults who use tobacco. The USPSTF recommends that clinicians ask all pregnant persons about tobacco use, advise them to stop using tobacco, and provide behavioral interventions for cessation to those who use tobacco.(129) National guidelines: The U.S. Preventive Services Task Force (USPSTF) concludes that there is insufficient evidence to determine the balance of benefits and harms of primary care interventions for tobacco cessation among school-age children and adolescents who already smoke because of a lack of adequately powered studies on behavioral counseling interventions and a lack of studies on medications. Clinical judgment should be used to decide how to best help youth who use tobacco.(130) Evidence: A systematic review of 33 trials with 21,600 participants found that incentives (eg, money, vouchers) improved smoking cessation rates at long-term follow-up (at least 6 months) in mixed-population studies. Ten trials of 2571 pregnant women found that women in the incentive-rewards group were more likely to stop smoking.(131) Evidence: A systematic review of 47 studies with more than 18,000 people showed improved smoking cessation with increased therapeutic support and nicotine replacement therapy (NRT) as compared with NRT alone.(103) Evidence: A review of 43 studies with more than 28,000 people found with moderate certainty that when no other support is available, written self-help materials help more people to stop smoking compared with getting no help at all.(132) Evidence: A meta-analysis of 10 randomized studies of 3655 participants using nicotine replacement therapy found that behavioral support focusing on adherence to smoking cessation medications can modestly improve adherence.(133) Evidence: A network meta-analysis of 33 systematic reviews with 250,563 participants found that behavioral support for smoking cessation can increase quit rates at 6 months or longer, with the strongest benefit for the provision of any type of counseling and guaranteed financial incentives.(134) Evidence: A systematic review of 136 trials of nicotine replacement therapy (NRT) with 64,640 people found that all forms of NRT made it more likely that a person's attempt to quit smoking would succeed. Their chances of stopping smoking increased by 50% to 60% with or without additional counseling. There is no evidence that NRT increases the risk of heart attacks. In pregnant women, evidence suggests that NRT can increase the chance of quitting at the time of delivery.(135) Evidence: A systematic review of 24 trials with 7279 participants found no evidence that adding exercise to smoking cessation support improves abstinence compared with support alone, but the evidence is insufficient to assess whether there is a modest benefit.(136) Evidence: A systematic review of 51 studies including 22,000 people who smoked tobacco found that cessation was more likely if smokers cut down their intake when using stop-smoking medication (eg, varenicline) or fast-acting nicotine replacement therapy vs quitting all at once.(101) Specialty Society: The American Thoracic Society recommendations for tobacco-dependent adults include using varenicline rather than a nicotine patch, using varenicline rather than bupropion, using varenicline rather than a nicotine patch in adults with a comorbid psychiatric condition, initiating varenicline in adults even if they are unready to quit, and using controller therapy for an extended treatment duration greater than 12 weeks.(106) References 1. [ A in Context Link 1 ] [B] A self-care plan tells you how to take care of your condition and when and where to get help for problems. [ B in Context Link 1 ] [C] A standard drink consists of 0.6 ounces (18 mL) of ethanol, which is contained in 12 ounces (360 mL) of beer (alcohol by volume (ABV) = 5%), 5 ounces (150 mL) of wine (ABV = 12%), and 1.5 ounces (45 mL) of spirits (ABV = 40%).(91) [ C in Context Link 1, 2 ] [D] Medication management includes medically supervised withdrawal treatment (unless contraindicated).(5) [ D in Context Link 1 ] [E] Telehealth may improve access to and coordination of mental health care. Patients with behavioral health diagnoses may use telehealth or telepsychiatry to access mental health support or care (eg, to get assistance with problem-solving techniques or ask about presenting symptoms or medication use).(137) [ E in Context Link 1 ] [F] Naloxone rescue training and education about opioid overdoses should be offered to the patient and family/caregivers.(32) [ F in Context Link 1, 2 ] [G] The timing of provider follow-up depends on the patient's disease severity, self-care complexity, patient/caregiver willingness and ability to provide patient care, and available social supports.(138) [ G in Context Link 1 ] [H] For patients on chronic opioid therapy, perioperative planning should include multimodal pain-management techniques (eg, nonpharmaceutical interventions, nonopioid medications).(47) Buprenorphine and methadone for the treatment of opioid use disorder may be continued in the perioperative period for most patients.(47) For patients taking buprenorphine, a perioperative plan should be instituted, whether it is holding buprenorphine, continuing it perioperatively, or transitioning to another medication.(139) Patients taking methadone may take methadone on the day of surgery and continue a home maintenance dose through the perioperative period (confirm home maintenance dose with prescribing provider).(47) [ H in Context Link 1 ] [I] Patients with persistent thoughts of suicide, homicide, or serious harm to self or another may need frequent monitoring for progression to planning or intention to act on their thoughts. Inpatient treatment may be appropriate for people with depression who are at significant risk of suicide, self-harm, or self-neglect.(63) Other patients who will reliably participate in monitoring and have supports and providers available may be safely treated with frequent monitoring in outpatient care.(65)(68) [ I in Context Link 1 ] [J] Restricting access to suicide methods includes removing firearms or other weapons from the home, keeping only immediate medication dosages available, removing materials that could be used for hanging or suffocation, and access restrictions for jumping (eg, closed-caption video monitoring, physical barrier to bridges).(41)(69)(140)(141) [ J in Context Link 1 ] [K] Adequate intake is used when the recommended daily allowance cannot be determined because of insufficient data on requirements.(92) [ K in Context Link 1 ] [L] Primary pharmacotherapy for tobacco dependence includes varenicline, bupropion, nicotine gum, nicotine inhaler, nicotine nasal spray, and nicotine patch. Nicotine replacement therapies (NRTs) should be used with caution for patients in the 2 weeks following myocardial infarction and for those with life-threatening arrhythmias or severe angina.(98)(129) Clinical appointment scheduling needed Goal: Clinical appointment scheduled and attended(1)(2)(3) Interventions: ASSIST: Identifying patient's goal priorities, willingness/ability to achieve goals, and preferences for communication and follow-up ASSIST: Goal setting that is specific, measurable, achievable, relevant, and with target completion time (eg, short/long- term, prioritized) ASSIST: Appointment scheduling with provider (eg, asking patient for available dates and times and calling office to schedule appointment) ASSIST: Preparation for appointment (eg, creating list of medications taken, list of all providers involved with care, and questions to ask the provider) ASSIST: Understanding when to schedule follow-up appointments or care EDUCATE: Follow-up appointments important to make and keep EDUCATE: What to bring to the appointment (eg, list of all medications taken, list of questions, all pill bottles) COORDINATE: Transportation to and from provider appointment, if needed COORDINATE: Reminder on day prior to appointment to triage any issues that may keep the patient from attending the appointment (eg, call, text) References 1. 10/10/24, 7:13 PM CCG - Blood Pressure Monitoring Plan of Care Problems P1. Blood pressure monitoring information needed Goal: Blood pressure self-monitoring improved Interventions: ASSIST: Identifying patient's goal priorities, willingness/ability to achieve goals, and preferences for communication and follow-up ASSIST: Goal setting that is specific, measurable, achievable, relevant, and with target completion time (eg, short/long- term, prioritized) ASSIST: Appointment scheduling with provider for evaluation of blood pressure problems or creation of self-care plan to include: Obtaining home blood pressure monitor with correct cuff size for patient Identifying blood pressure targets EDUCATE: Why blood pressure monitoring is important EDUCATE: When to check blood pressure EDUCATE: How to check blood pressure(1)(2)(3): Take blood pressure medication at same time each day. Avoid smoking, caffeine intake, and exercise for 30 minutes prior to taking blood pressure. Fit and position cuff properly (cuff should be appropriate size for patient). Do not put cuff over clothing. Support arm on flat surface at level of heart, with back supported and both feet on floor, for at least 5 minutes prior to taking blood pressure. Measure blood pressure in same arm each reading.[A](1) Blood pressure goal and the need to know own blood pressure from recent measurement Keeping a diary of blood pressure readings and reviewing diary with provider at appointments EDUCATE: When to call the provider: Blood pressure outside of provider's parameters There is a 10 mm Hg or greater difference in pressure between right and left arm.[B](4) COORDINATE: Home care referral, if indicated COORDINATE: Follow-up contact to evaluate blood pressure monitoring, referral status, and need for further assistance(5)(6) SEND: How to check your blood pressure (Illustrated) (Spanish) SEND: Blood pressure diary (Illustrated) (Spanish) References 1. Fetzer S. Vital signs. In: Perry AG, Potter PA, Ostendorf WR, editors. Nursing Interventions and Clinical Skills. 7th ed. Elsevier; 2020:101-132. [ Context Link 1, 2, 3 ] 2. Wermers R. Hypertension. In: Harding MM, Kwong J, Hagler D, Reinisch C, editors. Lewis's Medical-Surgical Nursing: Assessment and Management of Clinical Problems. 12th ed. St. Louis, MO: Mosby; 2023:797-818. [ Context Link 1 ] 3. Blood pressure measurement. In: Lippincott Nursing Procedures. 9th ed. Wolters Kluwer; 2023:80-83. [ Context Link 1 ] 4. Fang JC, O'Gara PT. History and physical examination: an evidence-based approach. In: Libby P, Bonow RO, Mann DL, Tomaselli GF, Bhatt DL, Solomon SD, editors. Braunwald's Heart Disease: A Textbook of Cardiovascular Medicine. 12th ed. Elsevier; 2022:123-140. [ Context Link 1, 2 ] 5. Care coordination and care transitions to support adherence. In: Perez R, Rogers S, Prince M, Fraser K, editors. Case Management Adherence Guidelines. 2020 ed. Case Management Society of America; 2022:43-56. [ Context Link 1 ] 6. Monitoring and evaluation. In: Gillingham DC, editor. Foundations of Case Management. Blue Bayou Press; 2021:51-52. [ Context Link 1 ] Footnotes [A] Patients may have up to a 10-point difference in readings between their two arms. For ongoing monitoring, blood pressure should be taken in the arm with the higher reading.(1) [ A in Context Link 1 ] [B] The difference in readings may be associated with subclavian artery disease, supravalvular aortic stenosis, aortic coarctation, or aortic dissection.(4) [ B in Context Link 1 ] MCG Health Chronic Care 28th Edition Copyright  2024 MCG Health, LLC All Rights Reserved https://careweb.careguidelines.com/ed28/ccg/iccg_245.htm?printview=1 2/3

[ Context Link 1 ] Footnotes [A] Percentiles may be obtained by using clinical growth charts, such as those available through the Centers for Disease Control and Prevention at www.cdc.gov/growthcharts. Percentiles rank the status of a child by showing what percentage of the reference group of the same age and sex the child would equal or exceed. They do not measure how well a child is growing because growth rate is measured over time and has a high level of variation between individuals. To track your child's growth, the Centers for Disease Control and Prevention provides a milestone tracker application at www.cdc.gov/ncbddd/actearly/milestones-app.html. 10/10/24, 7:11 PM CCG - Hospitalization Risk Assessment COORDINATE: Follow-up contact for evaluation of hospital risk factors, referral status, and need for further assistance(2)(11) Evidence Summary Quality standards: The American Case Management Association's Transitions of Care Standards indicate that processes should be in place to identify patients at risk for care transition problems, patients' health risks should be assessed at each care episode or transition, and a care management plan should be established and communicated across care settings.(12) Evidence: The literature discusses the creation and use of evidence-based risk models and algorithms, but results are mixed or inconclusive on effectiveness.(13)(14)(15)(16) Evidence: Many studies report higher readmission rates in patients with functional problems, multiple comorbidities (and associated polypharmacy and self-care needs), and social determinants of health.(2)(17)(18) Evidence: Several studies report that common clinical reasons for readmission are infection, gastrointestinal complications, bleeding, and fluid or electrolyte complications.(19)(20) Evidence: Several studies found that risk factors for psychiatric 30-day readmission include substance abuse, major psychiatric diagnosis (eg, bipolar disorder, schizophrenia), comorbid medical diagnosis, and longer length of stay in the first hospitalization.(21)(22) Transition consideration: Several studies demonstrated a reduction in 30-day hospital readmissions when the patient follow-up was within 7 days of hospital discharge.(23)(24)(25) Earlier follow-up (eg, within 48 hours after hospital discharge) may also help reduce hospital readmissions for high-risk or vulnerable patients.(2) Follow-up appointment attendance rates were higher when appointments were scheduled before hospital discharge.(26)(27) References 1. Medication adherence improvement needed Goal: Adherence to medication regimen improved[B] Interventions: ASSIST: Identifying patient's goal priorities, willingness/ability to achieve goals, and preferences for communication and follow-up ASSIST: Goal setting that is specific, measurable, achievable, relevant, and with target completion time (eg, short/long- term, prioritized) ASSIST: Appointment scheduling: Understanding preferences for medication use (eg, wanting to take fewer pills) to discuss with provider Understanding behavioral health aspects and how to get help ASSIST: Identifying and creating a plan to manage adherence issues (current or potential) that interfere with following prescribed medication regimen(3)(4): Resolving common medication adherence barriers (eg, difficulty remembering how or when to take, difficulty understanding labels, side effects, costs)(4) Identifying ways to improve following medication routine Overcoming communication barriers (eg, vision, language), as needed Simplifying medication regimen (eg, administration once or twice daily rather than more often)(4) Identifying medication discrepancies and resolving with provider Determining when refills are due and developing a plan for obtaining refills Working with pharmacy for filling prescriptions (eg, changing pills to liquids, identifying financial support, delivery) Follow-up appointment scheduling to evaluate treatment outcomes ASSIST: Communication with treating provider when another provider (eg, emergency or specialty provider) prescribes new medication EDUCATE: Medication routine improvement techniques(4): Organize medications (written charts, calendar trays with day and time of each dose, pill and bottle counts). Add medication regimen to daily habits (eg, with breakfast, before bedtime routine). 10/10/24, 7:11 PM CCG - Medication Adherence Medication purpose, dose, effects/side effects, and considerations (eg, managing missed doses, interactions with food/other drugs) Name of provider who ordered medication Administration techniques Side effects, related management, and when to contact provider Get emergency help: Adverse or allergic reactions (eg, wheezing, chest tightness, fever, itching, bad cough, blue skin color, hemorrhage, seizures, swelling of face, lips, tongue, or throat) Common side effects include gastrointestinal changes, rash, dizziness, and headache. Do not stop long-term medications abruptly due to risk of withdrawal symptoms. Medication safety (eg, proper storage and disposal)(11) Monitoring (eg, vital signs, laboratory checks) needed while taking medication Talk to provider before taking any over-the-counter medications, vitamins, minerals, or herbal or dietary supplements. Carry a list of medications at all times, particularly when visiting healthcare providers. 10/10/24, 7:11 PM CCG - Medication Adherence Evidence: In a randomized controlled trial, 5216 patients with a first-time statin prescription who did not pick up the medication within 1 week received a telephone reminder and personalized letter emphasizing the importance of therapy to prevent heart attack and stroke; 17% more patients in the intervention group filled the initial prescription within 2 weeks of the reminder call compared to patients in the control group.(20) Evidence: A systematic review of 29 studies found that social adversities (eg, food insecurity, housing instability) are associated with lower medication adherence.(6) Outcomes: Several studies support the role of the pharmacist in medication reconciliation to identify and resolve pharmacy-related problems and improve outcomes (eg, prevention of medication-related adverse events, discontinuation of inappropriate medication).
