Nutritional deficit for infant Problems P1. Healthy diet information needed for infant Goal: Infant's nutritional status optimized Interventions: ASSIST: Identifying caregiver's goal priorities, willingness/ability to achieve goals, and preferences for communication and follow-up ASSIST: Goal setting that is specific, measurable, achievable, relevant, and with target completion time (eg, short/long- term, prioritized) EDUCATE: Healthy body weight and height for infant based on age: Normal infant growth depends on gestational age.(1) Growth rate of full-term infants after 10 days of life is generally 20 to 30 grams/kg per day.(1) Weight gain for full-term infants is generally 680 grams per month.(2) Growth rate for preterm infants is from 10 to 30 grams/kg per day.(3) EDUCATE: Nutritional intake provision, with healthy, safe eating habits(2)(4)(5): Breast milk or formula for first 12 months Infants usually eat 8 to 12 times per day, with 2 to 4 hours between feedings.(6) Feed infant on cue (eg, rooting, pre-cry grimacing, fussing, crying). Crying is a late cue and may make feeding harder. Avoid giving infant cow's milk, low-iron milk (eg, goat, soy), and infant cereal.[A] Expect increase of feeding during growth spurts. EDUCATE: Feeding techniques(2)(4): Hold baby upright and close to the body when feeding. Burp infant frequently. Manage choking episodes. Calm baby before feeding (eg, dim lights, soothing touch, rocking, swaddling). Talk to infant while feeding. Do not prop bottle or enlarge bottle hole. Cleaning, storage, heating, and care of bottles and nipples EDUCATE: Monitor infant's output(4): Keep diary of oral intake and urine, stool, and emesis output. Count diapers and note results (eg, urine/stool, color, amount). Factors impacting stool frequency: type of nutrition (eg, breast milk, formula type) and age Check for problems (eg, more or less than usual, changes colors) EDUCATE: Solid food introduction(5): 4 to 6 months: usually ready developmentally https://careweb.careguidelines.com/ed28/ccg/iccg_mod_05290.htm?printview=1 2/6
10/10/24, 7:13 PM CCG - Nutritional Status, Infant Signs infant may be ready for solids: tongue thrust reflex (pushing food out of mouth) fades, coordinated swallowing, sits with support, and good head and neck control 6 to 12 months: usually master chewing and swallowing, able to handle finger foods, use cups and spoons, and try new tastes and textures Begin with one single-ingredient new food at a time; do not introduce new foods for 3 to 5 days to observe for allergy. Iron-fortified infant rice cereal least likely to cause allergy[B] Introduce soft moist foods in a variety of flavors and textures. It may take up to 15 attempts before infant accepts a particular food. EDUCATE: Oral care(5): Clean gums with plain water twice a day using clean moist washcloth or small-headed soft-bristled toothbrush. First tooth and all teeth when they erupt (6 to 10 months): brush with fluoridated toothpaste twice a day (after breakfast and before bed). Dental caries (tooth decay) causes (eg, frequent/prolonged feedings, intake of high-sugar sources (eg, fruit drinks, soft drinks, fruit juice)) Avoid feeding at night to prevent sugary fluids from pooling around the teeth to help prevent dental caries (tooth decay). Do not put infant to sleep with a bottle or sippy cup. Oral health of parent impacts infant's oral health: parents can transmit caries-promoting bacteria to their infant; oral health care for parent (eg, dental care, see dentist regularly, limit dietary sugar). EDUCATE: Feeding problem care; check and adjust(5): Feeding position (should be semi-erect, comfortable) Hunger cue responsiveness Environment; remove or relocate to limit distractions. Colic signs (eg, cries inconsolably for hours, passes a lot of gas); offer more-frequent feedings. EDUCATE: Resources: Women, Infants, and Children: www.fns.usda.gov/wic U.S. National Library of Medicine: www.nlm.nih.gov EDUCATE: When to get emergency help: Respiratory impairment (eg, gasping for air, choking, cyanosis, severe dyspnea)(7) Dehydration (eg, breathing hard, weight loss, eyes sunken, fontanelles sunken, irritability, not responding to sounds or sights)(8)(9) Aspiration (eg, coughing, choking, hypoxia, wheezing) EDUCATE: When to call the provider: Crying more for no reason or crying that cannot be soothed Feeding problems Urine problems (eg, frequency change, blood) Bowel problems (eg, frequency change, blood) Infection (eg, low-grade fever, vomiting, diarrhea) Caregiver cannot take care of baby anymore. COORDINATE: Dietitian referral, if indicated(10) COORDINATE: Home care referral, if indicated COORDINATE: Community services referral, as needed[C](11) COORDINATE: Social worker referral, as needed COORDINATE: Speech-language pathologist referral, if indicated COORDINATE: Swallowing evaluation referral, if indicated COORDINATE: Laboratory testing, if indicated COORDINATE: Care team meeting, if needed(12)(13) COORDINATE: Communication between care team members and care plan reconciliation(12)(14)(15) COORDINATE: Follow-up to evaluate infant's nutritional status, referral status, and need for further assistance(16)(17) SEND: How to breast feed (Spanish) SEND: Intake and output diary (Illustrated) (Spanish) Goal: Breast-feeding information understood(4) Interventions: EDUCATE: Breast-feeding frequency: First 2 weeks after birth: 10 to 12 times a day After first 2 weeks: 8 to 12 times a day for the next few months After 4 months: 6 to 12 times a day EDUCATE: Breast-feeding duration: recommended for 6 months exclusively, and can continue after 12 months EDUCATE: Breast-feeding techniques(5): Time needed for breast to let down Allow infant to finish one breast before offering other breast. https://careweb.careguidelines.com/ed28/ccg/iccg_mod_05290.htm?printview=1 3/6
10/10/24, 7:13 PM CCG - Nutritional Status, Infant Early weeks of life: feed 10 to 12 times a day and wake infant to try feeding if asleep for more than 4 hours. Length of feeding should not be restricted (20 to 45 minutes is generally enough). Frequent feeding establishes milk supply and keeps breasts from getting too full. Avoid artificial nipples (eg, pacifiers) until breast-feeding established (usually by 4 weeks).(4) EDUCATE: Breast care and problem symptoms (eg, infection signs) EDUCATE: Collecting and storing breast milk when unable to direct feed; time limits of frozen breast milk EDUCATE: Breast milk supplementation, as needed(18): First week of life: 400 International Units (IU) of vitamin D per day(4) Iron 1 mL/kg per day when food is introduced (around 4 months) Weaned infants can stop supplements when they get at least 1 quart (1 L) per day of vitamin D-fortified formula or whole milk.[A](5) EDUCATE: Mother's diet's effects on breast milk(5): Drink a glass of water (fluoridated) at every feeding; drink whenever thirsty. Limit caffeine (eg, coffee, tea, soft drinks) to 2 servings a day. Avoid drugs and alcohol. Possible maternal vitamin/mineral supplementation (eg, B12, iodine) if deficient (eg, vegan diet, chronic illness)(2) EDUCATE: Infant expected urine and stool frequency(2)(5)(19): Expect 6 to 8 wet diapers and 3 or 4 stools per day, and weight gain at appropriate rate. Stool frequency may decrease; by 6 weeks, stools may occur every 3 days. SEND: How to breast feed (Spanish) Goal: Formula feeding information understood(2)(4)(6) Interventions: ASSIST: Identifying food resource problems (eg, affording, accessibility) EDUCATE: Iron-fortified formula is preferred, unless contraindicated.[A] EDUCATE: Formula preparation: Birth to 1 month: average 20 ounces (600 mL) of formula per day Prepare and provide 2 ounces (60 mL) formula every 2 to 3 hours at first; provide more if infant still seems hungry. After 1 month: 24 to 31 ounces (720 to 930 mL) of formula per day Mix formula correctly (not lumpy or too watery). Use tap water or a safe water source that contains fluoride to mix powdered formula. Prepared formula (eg, not needing mix): cover and refrigerate after opening. Powdered formula: store at room temperature. Do not add foods (eg, cereal) to formula. Discard leftover formula when infant done feeding (do not reuse bottle). EDUCATE: Infant expected urine and stool frequency(19): Voiding 10 to 20 times per day Stool: 1 to 2 stools per day, or may stool only every other day P2. Potential nutrition problems for infant Goal: Infant's nutritional problems resolved Interventions: ASSIST: Identifying caregiver's goal priorities, willingness/ability to achieve goals, and preferences for communication and follow-up ASSIST: Goal setting that is specific, measurable, achievable, relevant, and with target completion time (eg, short/long- term, prioritized) ASSIST: Appointment scheduling ASSIST: Identifying food resource problems (eg, affording, accessibility) ASSIST: Creation of nutrition diary ASSIST: Assess for food or medication impacting nutritional status. EDUCATE: Nutritional recommendations for infant's health status EDUCATE: Food and drug interactions potentially impacting nutritional status EDUCATE: Feeding problem care(20)(21): Frequently try to feed; allow to feed as tolerated if interested in feeding. Nasal congestion problems: instill nasal saline drops and suction nares with bulb syringe before feeding. Oral rehydration solutions (eg, Infalyte or Pedialyte): use only if recommended by provider. Notify provider if intake or output outside expected parameters. EDUCATE: Monitor infant's output(4): Keep diary of oral intake and urine, stool, and emesis output. Count diapers and note results (eg, urine/stool, color, amount). Check that infant gets enough fluids. Check for problems (eg, more or less than usual, changes colors). COORDINATE: Community services referral, as needed(11) https://careweb.careguidelines.com/ed28/ccg/iccg_mod_05290.htm?printview=1 4/6

[ Context Link 1 ] Footnotes [A] Cow's milk should not be given to infants younger than 12 months.(5) [ A in Context Link 1, 2, 3 ] [B] Infants at high risk for developing allergies are those with at least one first-degree relative (ie, parent, sibling) with allergic disease. Breast-feeding exclusively for at least 4 months or using hydrolyzed formulas may prevent or delay the occurrence of atopic dermatitis, cow's milk allergy, and asthma in infancy and early childhood.(5) [ B in Context Link 1 ] [C] For infants with special needs, nutritional problems may be addressed with a nutritional program in an early intervention program.(5) [ C in Context Link 1, 2 ] MCG Health Chronic Care 28th Edition Copyright  2024 MCG Health, LLC All Rights Reserved Last Update: 3/14/2024 3:29:35 AM Build Number: 28.1.20240313235330.016020 https://careweb.careguidelines.com/ed28/ccg/iccg_mod_05290.htm?printview=1 6/6

10/10/24, 7:09 PM CCG - Readiness to Change COORDINATE: Follow-up evaluation for readiness to change with corresponding change in care plan P3. Information needed on how to make and sustain changes Goal: Information on how to make and sustain changes understood Interventions: ASSIST: Identifying patient's goal priorities, willingness/ability to achieve goals, and preferences for communication and follow-up ASSIST: Goal setting that is specific, measurable, achievable, relevant, and with target completion time (eg, short/long- term, prioritized) ASSIST: Appointment scheduling with provider to evaluate any clinical problems that may affect changes ASSIST: Support system (eg, family, friends) identification, enlistment, and education ASSIST: Setting up a self-care plan that patient feels they can follow(4)(5): Identifying patient's goal and desired outcomes Prioritizing goals and interventions Identifying stepwise approach to change Identifying and managing obstacles that may interfere with achieving goal Contacts for resources (eg, community resources, educational services) to help patient achieve their goal EDUCATE: Ways to help manage condition(7): How to take active role (eg, make decisions, incorporate care into daily routine, monitor condition) Stress management (eg, relaxation and coping techniques) Use support system to help. Ask for more information if condition or treatments are not understood. When and how to get help for problems COORDINATE: Behavioral health services referral, if indicated COORDINATE: Community services referral, as needed COORDINATE: Engagement and educational interventions appropriate for the patient's status or level of change readiness(9) COORDINATE: Home care referral, if indicated COORDINATE: Social worker referral, as needed(10) COORDINATE: Follow-up contact with patient to evaluate understanding and adherence to self-care plan, referral status, and need for further assistance(11)(12) Evidence Summary Quality standards: Standards indicate that the program should have a process for following up with patients on the status of referrals. (13) Evidence: In a prospective cohort study of 2155 high-risk patients, a single-level Patient Activation Measure (PAM) score increase was associated with an 8.3% lower follow-up healthcare cost.(14) References 1. 10/10/24, 7:08 PM CCG - Suicide or Personal Harm Risk ASSIST: National Suicide Prevention Lifeline: 1-800-273-8255 or call, text, or live chat 988 COORDINATE: Follow-up contact after behavioral health evaluation to review self-care plan and identify need for further assistance(3)(4) P2. Potential personal harm (ie, suicide or harm to others) risk present Goal: Personal harm (ie, suicide or harm to others) warning signs and action plan better understood Interventions: ASSIST: If patient is actively threatening harm to self or others, contact the crisis hotline or emergency services while maintaining communication with the patient.(1) Harming another person can be physical, emotional, or sexual. [A](2) ASSIST: Identifying patient's goal priorities, willingness/ability to achieve goals, and preferences for communication and follow-up ASSIST: Goal setting that is specific, measurable, achievable, relevant, and with target completion time (eg, short/long- term, prioritized) ASSIST: Appointment scheduling for evaluation of behavioral health status ASSIST: Identifying patient supports and setting up support system communication(1) ASSIST: Creation of an emergency plan so family and caregivers know steps to take if personal harm symptoms present(1) ASSIST: Identifying risks of suicide or harm to self or others(5)(6)(7): Self or family history of suicide or suicide attempts Recent stressful event (eg, recent exposure to another's suicide) or serious adverse childhood event History of being bullied Social isolation or withdrawal Severe functional impairments Spousal loss Discrimination experience (eg, lesbian, gay, bisexual, transgender) Demographic factors (eg, male, older age, non-Hispanic White or American Indian/Alaska Native race) Mental health disorders Triggers include physical pain, loss of support or respect from family or peers, interpersonal conflicts, painful memories, loneliness, helplessness, lack of self-worth. EDUCATE: Behavioral health crisis care(5)(8): Crisis care plan: turn off distractions (eg, TV, radio), seek emergent care, call hotline, call police if escalates; caregivers advised to not argue(9) Call police if crisis involves violence.(9) Keep the crisis hotline telephone number near the phone. Keep list of names, addresses, and emergency telephone numbers for medical help and support individuals (eg, neighbor, relative, close friend). EDUCATE: Suicide warning signs(10)(11): Depression (eg, feeling sad, hopeless, overwhelmed)(12) Giving away personal possessions, settling accounts, or other actions relating to disposition of estate in case of death Mood instability (eg, extreme or rapid changes) Reckless activities Sense of feeling trapped with no way out Sleep pattern changes (eg, hypersomnia, insomnia) Substance misuse (eg, increased use, use interferes with ADL/IADL) Suicidal or homicidal actions or plans(9) Social changes (eg, isolation, withdrawal) EDUCATE: Suicide prevention measures(13): Take any hint of suicide seriously, and seek immediate professional help. Encourage immediate intervention (eg, patient call crisis line, caregiver call provider).(11) Keep list of names, addresses, and emergency telephone numbers for medical help and support individuals (eg, neighbor, relative, close friend). Restrict access to methods of suicide.[B](11) EDUCATE: Suicidal ideation signs(11): Expressing suicidal thoughts or actions, or potential for harm to self or others Disclosing of suicide plan or plan to harm another Warning signs for suicide attempt (eg, writing suicide note, giving away valued possessions) Role playing (eg, standing on chair with noose) Recurrent thoughts of death (not just fear of dying) Available means to execute plan of harm to self or others EDUCATE: When to get emergency help(11): Suicidal or homicidal actions or plans https://careweb.careguidelines.com/ed28/ccg/iccg_307.htm?printview=1 2/4

10/10/24, 7:16 PM CCG - Urinary Catheter Management Plan of Care Problems P1. Urinary catheter self-care information needed Goal: Urinary catheter self-care plan followed Interventions: ASSIST: Identifying patient's goal priorities, willingness/ability to achieve goals, and preferences for communication and follow-up ASSIST: Goal setting that is specific, measurable, achievable, relevant, and with target completion time (eg, short/long- term, prioritized) ASSIST: Appointment scheduling ASSIST: Self-care plan creation between patient and provider (eg, goals, management, treating symptoms, getting help) ASSIST: Identifying ways to manage catheter needs in day-to-day life (eg, managing catheter outside the home) EDUCATE: Catheter care(1)(2): Practice good hygiene, with cleaning of catheter and around perineal skin. Measure urine and keep diary. Use clean collection bag every day. Keep catheter from being pulled. Privacy management (hiding catheter, managing straight catheter when away from home) Straight (intermittent) catheterization every 3 to 5 hours if patient dependent on catheterization to void Suprapubic catheter care, if indicated Bladder retraining after indwelling catheterization Urinary catheterization can cause urinary tract infections, particularly with indwelling catheters. EDUCATE: Catheter change schedule(3): Indwelling catheters: replace if leaking, blocked, or encrusted. Routine or scheduled changes are not recommended. Straight or condom catheters: replace per manufacturer's instructions. EDUCATE: When to get emergency help(4): Urine pattern change, sudden (eg, not making urine) Abdominal pain, severe, unrelieved by analgesics Bladder overdistended (eg, pounding headache, sweating for no reason, cold hands or feet, feeling anxious) EDUCATE: When to call the provider(5): Bowel or bladder dysfunction Catheter insertion problems Urinary problems (eg, pain, frequency change, blood)(1) Urinary retention after catheter removal(3) Urine problems (eg, cloudy, dark yellow, or brown) Urine stones COORDINATE: Laboratory testing, if indicated COORDINATE: Outpatient imaging, tests, or procedures, if indicated COORDINATE: Medical equipment and supplies, if indicated COORDINATE: Home care referral, if indicated COORDINATE: Community services referral, as needed COORDINATE: Social worker referral, as needed(6) COORDINATE: Urologist referral, if indicated COORDINATE: Communication between care team members and care plan reconciliation(7)(8)(9) COORDINATE: Follow-up evaluation of catheter, referral status, and need for further assistance(10)(11) SEND: How to wash your hands (Illustrated) (Spanish) SEND: Tips for self-catheterization (Spanish) SEND: How to use and care for a Foley catheter (Illustrated) (Spanish) SEND: Tips for using an indwelling catheter (Spanish) SEND: How to self-catheterize for a female anatomy (Illustrated) (Spanish) SEND: How to self-catheterize for a male anatomy (Illustrated) (Spanish) SEND: How to use a condom catheter (Illustrated) (Spanish) SEND: Suprapubic catheter care - Stoma care (Illustrated) (Spanish) SEND: Suprapubic catheter care - Urine bag care (Illustrated) (Spanish) Goal: Urinary tract problems prevented Interventions: https://careweb.careguidelines.com/ed28/ccg/iccg_170.htm?printview=1 2/3
10/10/24, 7:16 PM CCG - Urinary Catheter Management ASSIST: Identifying patient's goal priorities, willingness/ability to achieve goals, and preferences for communication and follow-up ASSIST: Goal setting that is specific, measurable, achievable, relevant, and with target completion time (eg, short/long- term, prioritized) ASSIST: Appointment scheduling EDUCATE: Urinary tract infection prevention measures(1)(2): Proper hand hygiene Proper external skin hygiene Keep catheter and tubing from kinking. Keep bag below level of bladder. Empty bag regularly. Drink 8 to 12 cups (1.9 to 2.9 L) of fluid per day.(12) EDUCATE: Bladder care measures(2): Prevent bladder from becoming overdistended (generally less than 400 mL). Maintain steady fluid intake (within dietary limits). 10/10/24, 7:15 PM CCG - Venous Thromboembolism ASSIST: Identifying patient's goal priorities, willingness/ability to achieve goals, and preferences for communication and follow-up ASSIST: Goal setting that is specific, measurable, achievable, relevant, and with target completion time (eg, short/long- term, prioritized) ASSIST: Appointment scheduling for evaluation of blood clotting medication, if needed EDUCATE: Clotting prevention medication information: Reason for taking blood clot medication, dosing schedule, and how to manage missed doses Medication problems (eg, side effects, access, administration) Take at same time each day. Medical ID indicating blood clot medication taken(3) Duration of therapy depends on patient's problems. Tell all healthcare providers of use.(3) Tell provider about excessive bleeding signs (eg, bleeding gums, nosebleeds, many bruises). Tell provider about pain in the legs while taking this medication. Get help right away for chest pain or trouble breathing. Medication-related safety Discuss medication with provider if pregnant or plan to become pregnant and taking a blood thinner.[A](3) EDUCATE: Ways to stay safe while taking blood clot medications(3)(4): Avoid foods that may cause diarrhea or upset stomach, or rapid changes in diet.(12)(13) Floss gently if it does not cause bleeding. Blow nose gently. Use lotion to prevent drying and cracking of skin. Prevent constipation with stool softeners as prescribed. Keep fingernails and toenails short and smooth using nail clipper and file. Use water-soluble lubricants during sexual activity to decrease risk of abrasions. Try not to cause injury (eg, shave with electric razor, use soft-bristled toothbrush, do not use sharp instruments or play high-contact sports). EDUCATE: Low-molecular-weight heparin self-care, if appropriate(13)(14): May be used during the transition from hospital to outpatient care Heparin is administered by subcutaneous injection. EDUCATE: Warfarin, if appropriate(4)(14): Dose is monitored using regular blood testing of INR. Warfarin effects (blood thinning) changed by diet. INR can be lowered by eating more than normal amount of foods containing vitamin K. Warfarin interacts with many medications and herbal products. Alcohol may impact INR laboratory values and blood clotting ability. Limit alcohol intake to no more than 1 or 2 drinks a week. 10/10/24, 7:15 PM CCG - Venous Thromboembolism Distended neck veins Fainting EDUCATE: Deep venous thrombosis (DVT) signs(1)(4)(18)(19): Localized tenderness or redness along the area of the affected vein Leg edema, pitting possible Calf swelling 3 cm or larger than asymptomatic side Dilated superficial veins (non-varicose) COORDINATE: Communication between care team members and care plan reconciliation(5)(6)(7) COORDINATE: Follow-up contact to evaluate blood clot information and need for further assistance(8)(9) Evidence Summary Specialty society: The American College of Chest Physicians and the National Institute for Health and Care Excellence (NICE) do not recommend the use of compression stockings to prevent post-thrombotic syndrome following proximal deep venous thrombosis.(20) (21) Evidence: A systematic review of 20 randomized controlled trials found that graduated compression stockings are effective in reducing the risk of deep venous thrombosis (DVT) after general and orthopedic surgery, based on high-quality evidence. A reduced risk of proximal DVT is probable with the use of graduated compression stockings, based on moderate-quality evidence; however, there remains a lack of evidence of the effectiveness of graduated compression stockings in medical patients.(22) References 1. ## Patient Education Documents

### pted_03409

What you need to know - Heart transplant What you need to know - Heart transplant This handout tells you what you need to know about a heart transplant to live better and avoid serious problems. What is it? • A heart transplant is a surgery to take out the diseased heart and replace it with a healthy heart. • You must take medicines for the rest of your life. – These medicines help keep your body from rejecting the heart transplant. – Your body may reject the heart transplant any time if you stop taking your medicines. What types of care may I need? • Goals for care – Feel better and stay active – Treat and prevent problems – Better quality of life • Equipment or supplies – Compression stockings – Weight scale – Wound care supplies • Medicines – Erythropoiesis-stimulating agents (ESAs) help the body make more red blood cells. Page 1 of 4
What you need to know - Heart transplant What you need to know - Heart transplant – Immunosuppressives reduce the body's immune system response. – Steroids reduce swelling and treat bad flare ups. • Home healthcare • Community resources What follow-up care do I need? • Surgeon or transplant team visits • Tests, like blood, urine, EKG (electrocardiogram), ultrasound, or x-rays • Heart biopsies • Cancer screenings, like cervix, breast, colon, prostate, or skin • Bone density tests for osteoporosis • Vaccinations that are not made from live viruses • Annual eye exams • Dental care What can I do? • Managing a heart transplant includes following a daily care plan. See the Daily care for your condition - Organ transplant handout. • Make and keep all doctor visits. • Take your medicine as your doctor told you. • Stay away from crowds and people who are sick with colds or flu. • Protect your skin when you go outside. Cover your skin with clothes or wear sunscreen that is SPF30 or higher. • Keep your weight at a healthy number. • Take care of other health problems that you have. • Make lifestyle changes, like stop smoking, eat healthy foods, and be active. Page 2 of 4
What you need to know - Heart transplant What you need to know - Heart transplant • Learn to manage stress. Keep in touch with family and friends. Ask for help if you need it. • Know when to call for help. – Get emergency help right away if you think you are having a heart attack or stroke. – Contact your transplant doctor right away if you show signs of rejection. – See the When to get help - Organ transplant handout. Where can I learn more? • Review some Internet resources. – American Heart Association. Go to: www.heart.org – National Heart, Lung, and Blood Institute. National Institutes of Health. Go to: www.nhlbi.nih.gov – Smokefree.gov. National Institutes of Health. Go to: www.smokefree.gov • Talk to your doctor about what you learn. This information is general in nature and may not apply to you or your condition. Talk to your doctor or nurse about how to best use this information and how to take care of your medical condition. Be sure to ask your doctor or nurse any questions you might have. This information sheet is not intended to provide or replace professional medical advice, diagnosis, or treatment. ### pted_03411

What you need to know - Liver transplant What you need to know - Liver transplant This handout tells you what you need to know about a liver transplant to live better and avoid serious problems. What is it? • A liver transplant is a surgery to take out the diseased liver. There are 2 types of liver transplants. – The whole liver is replaced with a healthy liver. – Part of the liver is replaced with a piece of a healthy liver. • You must take medicines for the rest of your life. – These medicines help keep your body from rejecting the liver transplant. – Your body may reject the liver transplant any time if you stop taking your medicines. What types of care may I need? • Goals for care – Feel better and stay active – Treat and prevent problems – Better quality of life • Equipment or supplies – Compression stockings – Weight scale – Wound care supplies • Medicines Page 1 of 4
What you need to know - Liver transplant What you need to know - Liver transplant – Erythropoiesis-stimulating agents (ESAs) help the body make more red blood cells. – Immunosuppressives reduce the body's immune system response. – Steroids reduce swelling and treat bad flare ups. • Home healthcare • Community resources What follow-up care do I need? • Surgeon or transplant team visits • Tests, like blood, urine, ultrasound, or x-rays • Cancer screenings, like cervix, breast, colon, prostate, or skin • Bone density tests for osteoporosis • Vaccinations that are not made from live viruses • Annual eye exams • Dental care What can I do? • Managing a liver transplant includes following a daily care plan. See the Daily care for your condition - Organ transplant handout. • Make and keep all doctor visits. • Take your medicine as your doctor told you. • Stay away from crowds and people who are sick with colds or flu. • Protect your skin when you go outside. Cover your skin with clothes or wear sunscreen that is SPF30 or higher. • Keep your weight at a healthy number. • Take care of other health problems, like depression or high blood pressure (hypertension). Page 2 of 4
What you need to know - Liver transplant What you need to know - Liver transplant • Make lifestyle changes, like stop smoking, eat healthy foods, and be active. • Learn to manage stress. Keep in touch with family and friends. Ask for help if you need it. • Know when to call for help. – Get emergency help right away if you think you are having a heart attack or stroke. – Contact your transplant doctor right away if you show signs of rejection. – See the When to get help - Organ transplant handout. Where can I learn more? • Review some Internet resources. – American Liver Foundation. Go to: www.liverfoundation.org – MedlinePlus. National Institutes of Health. ### pted_03446

Medicines for constipation These medicines help treat constipation (hard stools). What medicine do I take? • There are different types of constipation medicines. – Bulk forming laxatives, such as methylcellulose, polycarbophil, and psyllium – Lubricant laxatives, such as mineral oil – Osmotic laxatives, such as glycerin suppositories, magnesium hydroxide, and polyethylene glycol – Stimulant laxatives, such as bisacodyl, castor oil, and sennosides – Stool softeners, such as docusate sodium • Talk to your doctor or nurse if you do not know the names of your medicine. What does this medicine do? • Helps your bowels move • Keeps you from having problems with constipation – Bulking agents help your body make more stool and make stool easier to pass. – Lubricant laxatives make stool slick to make it easier to pass. – Osmotic laxatives help make the stool softer and easier to pass. – Stimulant laxatives make stool move faster through the intestines. This type of laxative is not supposed to be taken regularly. – Stool softeners help make stool soft and easier to pass. How do I take this medicine? • Ask your doctor if you have any questions about taking these medicines. • Constipation medicine can be taken many ways. – Most constipation medicines are pills or liquids taken by mouth. Page 1 of 3
Medicines for constipation – Powder forms can be mixed with water or other liquids. – Suppositories are taken in the rear end (rectum). • Take your medicine as directed. – Some constipation medicines should be taken first thing in the morning or before bedtime. – Never take more than the recommended dose. – Ask your doctor or pharmacist if you are unsure how to take the medicine. • Drink lots of water or other fluids while taking medicines for constipation. • Stop taking laxatives when constipation improves. What are the side effects? • Common side effects: – Stomach (abdominal) cramps – Bloating or gas – Loose stools (diarrhea) • Serious side effects: – Low body fluids (dehydration) – Muscle cramps or pain – Weakness – Severe stomach cramps – Very loose stools (bad diarrhea) that last for more than 1 day – Abnormal heartbeat (arrhythmia) – Blood potassium level that is too high or too low • Some laxatives can cause problems with how your body absorbs nutrients. This can lead to serious problems, such as heart problems, weakness, confusion, or seizures (body shaking, staring, eyes rolling). Page 2 of 3
Medicines for constipation Do I need to take special steps when taking this medicine? • Talk to your doctor about what type of medicine to take for constipation and when to take it. – Some constipation medicines should not be taken with other medicines or supplements. – Do not give a child a constipation medicine without asking the child's doctor if it is OK. • Take constipation medicines only when you need to. – Do not take them regularly unless your doctor tells you to. – Regular use of laxatives can change your body's ability to absorb vitamin D and calcium. – Sodium phosphate laxatives should only be used once a day and for no longer than 3 days. • Talk to your doctor if you take a constipation medicine and still do not have a bowel movement. This information is general in nature and may not apply to you or your condition. Talk to your doctor or nurse about how to best use this information and how to take care of your medical condition. Be sure to ask your doctor or nurse any questions you might have. This information sheet is not intended to provide or replace professional medical advice, diagnosis, or treatment. ### pted_703

What you need to know - Hematopoietic stem cell transplant (bone marrow transplant) What you need to know - Hematopoietic stem cell transplant This handout tells you what you need to know about a hematopoietic stem cell (bone marrow) transplant to live better and avoid serious problems. What is it? • Bone marrow is the soft tissue inside the bones that makes blood cells. – All blood cells are made from stem cells found in the bone marrow. – New blood cells are always needed to replace old blood cells. • Stem cells can get damaged or destroyed from different things. – Blood conditions, like aplastic anemia, sickle cell disease, or thalassemia – Bone marrow cancers, like leukemia, lymphoma, or multiple myeloma – Cancer treatments, like chemotherapy or radiation therapy – Chemicals that you come in contact with – Immune system problems – Infections – Family history (genetics) of conditions that affect bone marrow • Some people use other names for a hematopoietic stem cell transplant. – Bone marrow transplant – Stem cell transplant – Nonmyeloablative transplant – Mini-transplant • A hematopoietic stem cell transplant replaces damaged or destroyed bone marrow with healthy bone marrow stem cells. Page 1 of 4
What you need to know - Hematopoietic stem cell transplant (bone marrow transplant) What you need to know - Hematopoietic stem cell transplant – It takes 10 to 28 days for the new stem cells to start to work. – It takes 3 months for the stem cells to work well. What types of care may I need? • Goals for care – Feel better and stay active – Treat and prevent problems – Better quality of life • Medicines – Erythropoiesis-stimulating agents (ESAs) help the body make more red blood cells. – Immunosuppressives reduce the body's immune system response. – Steroids reduce swelling and treat bad flare ups. • Home healthcare • Community resources What follow-up care do I need? • Surgeon or transplant team visits • Tests, like blood, urine, or stools • Cancer screenings, like cervix, breast, colon, prostate, or skin • Bone density tests for osteoporosis • Vaccinations that are not made from live viruses • Annual eye exams • Dental care Page 2 of 4
What you need to know - Hematopoietic stem cell transplant (bone marrow transplant) What you need to know - Hematopoietic stem cell transplant What can I do? • Managing a hematopoietic stem cell transplant includes following a daily care plan. See the Daily care for your condition - Organ transplant handout. • Make and keep all doctor visits. • Take your medicine as your doctor told you. • Stay away from crowds and people who are sick with colds or flu. • Protect your skin when you go outside. Cover your skin with clothes or wear sunscreen that is SPF30 or higher. • Protect yourself from injury. • Keep your weight at a healthy number. • Take care of your underlying condition, like sickle cell disease. • Make lifestyle changes, like stop smoking, eat healthy foods, and be active. • Learn to manage stress. Keep in touch with family and friends. Ask for help if you need it. • Know when to call for help. – Get emergency help right away if you think you are having a heart attack or stroke. – Contact your transplant doctor right away if you show signs of rejection. – See the When to get help - Organ transplant handout. Where can I learn more? • Review some Internet resources. – Blood & Marrow Transplant Information Network. Go to: www.bmtinfonet.org – National Bone Marrow Transplant Link. Go to: www.nbmtlink.org – National Cancer Institute. National Institutes of Health. Go to: www.cancer.gov or call 1-800-4-CANCER or 1-800-422-6237 Page 3 of 4
What you need to know - Hematopoietic stem cell transplant (bone marrow transplant) What you need to know - Hematopoietic stem cell transplant – Smokefree.gov. National Institutes of Health. Go to: www.smokefree.gov • Talk to your doctor about what you learn. This information is general in nature and may not apply to you or your condition. Talk to your doctor or nurse about how to best use this information and how to take care of your medical condition. Be sure to ask your doctor or nurse any questions you might have. This information sheet is not intended to provide or replace professional medical advice, diagnosis, or treatment. ### pted_747

Managing your medicines This handout tells you how to manage your medicines to take them safely and get the most out of them. Choose the tips that you think will work best for you. Know your medicines. 1 L 2 3 4 IS IN O P R IL • Know what each medicine does (for example, lowers blood pressure). • Keep track of how each medicine looks (for example, white tablet). Read the label on the medicine bottle to find out important information: 20mg 1. Name and strength of medicine (for example, Lisinopril 20 mg) 2. When you should take it (for example, once a day in the morning) 3. How you should take it (for example, with water) 4. Doctor who prescribed it Keep track of your medicines. Take your medicines at the right times. • Fill a pill box with the medicines you need for the week. • Keep your medicines in the original bottles until you put them in the pill box. • Take your medicines at the same time every day. Use a chart, calendar, or timer to help you remember. Page 1 of 5
Managing your medicines Avoid problems with your medicines. • Make a list of all the medicines you take. – Include vitamins, herbs, and all the medicines you buy at the grocery or drug store. – Make changes to the list whenever your medicines change. Carry a copy of the list in your wallet. – Give your list to all your doctors, dentists, and pharmacists. • Ask your doctor or pharmacist what you should do if you miss a dose. Find out about the side effects for each of your medicines and which ones are serious. • Do not stop taking your medicines without first talking to your doctor. Re- order in plenty of time so you do not run out. • Use one pharmacy for all your prescriptions. Get a 3 month supply if you can. Sign up to have medicines you take regularly filled automatically. • Check with your doctor or pharmacist before taking any vitamins, herbs, or medicines you buy at the grocery or drug store. • Never share your medicines with anyone else. Never take someone else's medicine. • Ask your doctor if you should carry an ID card or wear an alert bracelet for any of your medicines. Page 2 of 5
Managing your medicines • Your doctor may give you a medicine called naloxone. It will help you if you take too much pain medicine by mistake. Tell someone where you keep it and how to use it. Store your medicines safely. • Keep medicines out of the reach of children. • Keep your medicines in a cool dry place where you can find them easily. Store medicines in the refrigerator if the label says to. • Do not store pills or capsules in the bathroom or next to the stove. • Have a box or container for each person in your house. Label the box or container with the name of the person in large bold letters. • Place that person's medicines in the original bottle in the box. Put them in the order that they are taken each day. • Write on the cap with a permanent marker which medicines are to be taken with water, with food, or on an empty stomach. You could also write on the cap what you should not take your medicine with, like NO MILK. • Use a plastic cup in the medicine cabinet to hold small tubes of creams, balms, or topical ointments like antibiotics. Page 3 of 5
Managing your medicines • Bring medicines you need during the day with you when you go out. When traveling, put medicines in your carry- on bags, not in checked luggage. Dispose of your medicines properly. • Clean out the medicine cabinet at least once a year. • Remove all medicines that have expired dates. Include medicines in tubes and medicines you bought at the grocery or drug store that were not ordered by your doctor. • Remove all medicines that you no longer take. Check with your pharmacist about a local take-back program. If you have one in your area, take your expired medicines there. If no medicine take-back program is in your area, follow these steps to dispose of most medicines: 1. Mix medicines (do not crush tablets or capsules) with something that tastes bad, like kitty litter or coffee grounds. 2. Place the mixture in a sealed plastic bag. 3. Throw the bag in your trash. 4. Do not put pain medicines in this bag or in your trash. Pain medicines should be flushed down the toilet or sink to make sure no person or animal swallows the medicine by mistake. Page 4 of 5
Managing your medicines This information is general in nature and may not apply to you or your condition. Talk to your doctor or nurse about how to best use this information and how to take care of your medical condition. Be sure to ask your doctor or nurse any questions you might have. This information sheet is not intended to provide or replace professional medical advice, diagnosis, or treatment. ### pted_830

Daily care for your condition - Organ transplant Daily care for your condition - Organ transplant This handout tells you what you need to do to care for your organ transplant and work with your doctor to stay healthy. Follow your self-care plan. • The self-care plan is a list of what you need to do every day to manage your care after the transplant. • It tells you the signs that your condition is getting worse and when to take action right away. • Ask your doctor or nurse if there are things you do not understand. • Make and keep all doctor visits. Take your medicine as your doctor told you. Rx • Take your medicines the right way after your transplant. – Do not take all your medicines at one time. The doses are spread out to keep the right levels in your body. – Tell your doctor or transplant team if you miss a dose. – Ask the transplant team before taking vitamins, herbs, or medicines you buy at the store. • Know why you take each medicine. • Know the medicine side effects to watch out for. • Know what to do if you miss a dose. • Keep a list of all the medicine you take to show your doctor. • Get the follow-up tests that your doctor orders. Page 1 of 5
Daily care for your condition - Organ transplant Daily care for your condition - Organ transplant Do health checks at home. • You may need to check your blood pressure every day. – Wait 30 minutes after exercise before you take your blood pressure. – Take your blood pressure in the same arm each time. – Write down your blood pressure reading in a diary to show your doctor. • Follow a plan if your blood pressure is too high or low. Seek medical care right away if: – It goes higher than _________ / _________. – It goes lower than _________ / _________. • Know your target blood pressure range. Work to keep it at this level. – The top number (systolic) can range from _________ to _________. – The bottom number (diastolic) can range from _________ to _________. • You may need to check your heart rate every day. – Sit quietly for 10 minutes before you start. – Place 2 fingers lightly on the artery on the inside of your wrist. Do not use your thumb. – Using a clock or watch with a second hand, count the beats you feel for 60 seconds. – Write down your heart rate in a diary to show your doctor. • Know your target heart rate range. – It is too high when it is _________. – It is too low when it is _________. – Take steps to keep your heart rate under control. – Write down your heart rate in a diary to show your doctor. Page 2 of 5
Daily care for your condition - Organ transplant Daily care for your condition - Organ transplant • You may need to weigh yourself every day. • Take your temperature every day. Keep a diary of your temperatures to show your doctor. • Bring a diary of your health checks to your doctor visits. Follow the eating plan as your doctor told you. • You should eat a balance of healthy foods after a transplant. – Lean protein, like chicken (no skin), tofu, or eggs – Fiber, like whole grain cereals, vegetables, and fruits to keep stools from getting too hard (constipation) – Limit foods with a lot of fat, cholesterol, salt (sodium), or sugar. – Take a multivitamin, if your doctor says it is OK. – Avoid alcohol, like beer, wine, hard liquor, and mixed drinks. – You may not be able to drink grapefruit juice with certain medicines. Ask your doctor or pharmacist if there are things you should not eat or drink. • Keep from catching or spreading germs from food. – Wash hands before and after handling and eating food. – Wash fruits and vegetables with water before eating. – Only eat chicken, pork, and beef when cooked all the way through. – Do not eat food that was left at room temperature for more than 2 hours. • Avoid foods that could make you sick, such as: – Raw or undercooked meat, eggs, and fish – Unpasteurized milk or cheese (like feta, brie, camembert, Roquefort, and Mexican soft cheese) – Raw sprouts, like bean and alfalfa Page 3 of 5
Daily care for your condition - Organ transplant Daily care for your condition - Organ transplant • Talk to your doctor about what you should eat. – Calorie needs are based on my activity level and the need to gain or lose weight. – I should eat ____________ calories a day. Take steps for a healthy lifestyle. • Keep active and exercise. – Get extra rest while you are healing. Try to be a little more active each day. Rest when you feel you need to. – Talk to your doctor about what exercise is best for you and make it part of your day. – Do not drive or return to work until your doctor says it is OK. • Prevent blood clots in your legs. – Keep your legs propped up while lying down. – Avoid sitting for long periods of time. If you do, stretch your legs as much as you can. Do not cross your legs while sitting. – Do not wear socks with tight bands that leave marks on your legs. – Wear compression stockings every day if your doctor told you to. – Take blood thinner medicine if your doctor orders it. • Get a medical ID bracelet, necklace, or card for your wallet that says you had a transplant. Keep it with you at all times. • Lose weight if you are overweight. • Learn healthy ways to manage stress. • Do not smoke. • Do not drink alcohol. • Do not use drugs or misuse medicine. Page 4 of 5
Daily care for your condition - Organ transplant Daily care for your condition - Organ transplant • Get your shots (vaccines) each year, like the flu shot. Know the signs that your condition is getting worse. • Know when to call for emergency help. – You think you are having a heart attack or stroke. – You have very bad breathing problems. – You have any signs of a blood clot in your leg. • Know when to call your doctor or transplant team. – See the When to get help - Organ transplant handout. This information is general in nature and may not apply to you or your condition. Talk to your doctor or nurse about how to best use this information and how to take care of your medical condition. Be sure to ask your doctor or nurse any questions you might have. This information sheet is not intended to provide or replace professional medical advice, diagnosis, or treatment. ### pted_831

What you need to know - Organ transplant What you need to know - Organ transplant This handout tells you what you need to know about an organ transplant to live better and avoid serious problems. What is it? • An organ transplant replaces a badly damaged organ with a healthy one from another person. • The organ may come from a living person or from someone who has died. • Transplants can be done for many organs in the body, like bowel (intestine) or pancreas. • You must take medicines for the rest of your life. – These medicines help keep your body from rejecting the transplant. – Your body may reject the transplant any time if you stop taking your medicines. • To learn more about types of transplants, see one of these handouts: – What you need to know - Heart transplant – What you need to know - Hematopoietic stem cell transplant (bone marrow transplant) – What you need to know - Kidney transplant – What you need to know - Liver transplant – What you need to know - Lung transplant What types of care may I need? • Goals for care – Feel better and stay active Page 1 of 4
What you need to know - Organ transplant What you need to know - Organ transplant – Treat and prevent problems – Better quality of life • Equipment or supplies – Compression stockings – Weight scale – Wound care supplies • Medicines – Erythropoiesis-stimulating agents (ESAs) help the body make more red blood cells. – Immunosuppressives reduce the body's immune system response. – Steroids reduce swelling and treat bad flare ups. • Home healthcare • Community resources What follow-up care do I need? • Surgeon or transplant team visits • Tests, like blood and urine tests • Cancer screenings, like cervix, breast, colon, prostate, or skin • Bone density tests for osteoporosis • Vaccinations that are not made from live viruses • Annual eye exams • Dental care Page 2 of 4
What you need to know - Organ transplant What you need to know - Organ transplant What can I do? • Follow your daily care plan. See the Daily care for your condition - Organ transplant handout. • Make and keep all doctor visits. • Take your medicine as your doctor told you. • Stay away from crowds and people who are sick with colds or flu. • Protect your skin when you go outside. Cover your skin with clothes or wear sunscreen that is SPF30 or higher. • Keep your weight at a healthy number. • Take care of other health problems that you have. • Make lifestyle changes, like stop smoking, eat healthy foods, and be active. • Learn to manage stress. Keep in touch with family and friends. Ask for help if you need it. • Know when to call for help. – Get emergency help right away if you think you are having a heart attack or stroke. – Contact your transplant doctor right away if you show signs of rejection. – See the When to get help - Organ transplant handout. Where can I learn more? • Review some Internet resources. – MedlinePlus. National Institutes of Health. Go to: www.medlineplus.gov – Smokefree.gov. National Institutes of Health. What you need to know - Organ transplant What you need to know - Organ transplant – United Network for Organ Sharing (UNOS). Go to: www.unos.org • Talk to your doctor about what you learn.
